hyperkalemia, the use of succinylcholine is best avoided after 24 hours following a burn injury.

The time course of abnormal muscle membrane function corresponds with that of the healing process. Once normal skin has regrown and any infection subsides, the normal acetylcholine receptor populations appear to return.370 Although normal responses to succinylcholine have been demonstrated in burned patients studied 3 years after injury,370 the actual length of time during which a patient with a burn injury may be at risk for the hyperkalemic response is not well defined. A conservative guideline therefore would be to avoid the use of succinylcholine in patients 24 hours after a thermal injury and for at least 1 or 2 years after the burned skin has healed.

USE OF NEUROMUSCULAR BLOCKING DRUGS AND WEAKNESS SYNDROMES IN CRITICALLY ILL PATIENTS ​

NMBDs are frequently used in conjunction with sedatives and analgesics in ICUs. The indications for the use of NMBDs in the ICU are outlined in Box 24.1. Few available data support their use, and evidence for a beneficial effect on pulmonary function or patient oxygenation is inconclusive.373 However, a multicenter, double- blind trial showed that, in a subset of patients with acute respiratory distress syndrome, early and short- term administration of cisatracurium for 48 hours could be beneficial.374 Half of the patients in the placebo group in that study received one or more doses of cisatracurium. The study was underpowered, and the effect on mortality was statistically borderline, with no between- group difference in crude mortality rate. Yet, nondepolarizing NMBDs are sometimes used in ICU patients. Of particular concern in intensive- care settings is the risk that paralyzed patients receive inadequate analgesia and sedation.375 The reason for this concern may be that ICU nurses and physicians are unfamiliar with the pharmacology of the NMBDs.375,376 For instance, pancuronium was thought to be an anxiolytic by 50% to 70% of ICU nurses and house staff, and 5% to 10% thought it was an analgesic.375 In the United Kingdom, the erroneous use of NMBDs as sedatives in intensive care was not uncommon in the 1980s.377 Approximately 96% of ICU patients received NMBDs to aid mechanical ventilation in 1980. By 1986, their use had decreased to 16% of ventilated patients.377 Currently, intensivists are aware of the side effects and

原始表格與符號（PDF 第 32 頁）

Box 24.1 Reported Indications for the Use of Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs in the Intensive Care Unit ​

Facilitation of mechanical ventilation Facilitation of endotracheal intubation Enabling patient to tolerate mechanical ventilation High pulmonary inflation pressures (e.g., acute respiratory distress syndrome) Hyperventilation for increased intracranial pressure Facilitation of therapeutic or diagnostic procedures Tetanus Status epilepticus Reduction of oxygen consumption Abolishing shivering Reduction of work of breathing

generally avoid administration of NMBDs to critically ill ICU patients. Clinical practice guidelines for management of critically ill adults who require neuromuscular paralysis have been published.378 Specific indications for the use of NMBDs in the ICU setting include severe, refractory hypoxemia; suppression of shivering during therapeutic hypothermia after cardiac arrest; elimination of unwanted movement in patients with status asthmaticus, high intracranial or intra- abdominal pressure, massive hemoptysis, or to facilitate short procedures such as bronchoscopy or endoscopy; and acute respiratory failure in patients who require emergent tracheal intubation.378

A prolonged ICU stay during critical illness is associated with disorders of neuromuscular function that contribute to morbidity, length of hospital stay, weaning difficulties, and prolonged rehabilitation.379 The complications of longterm administration of NMBDs in the ICU are outlined in Box 24.2. In the ICU, duration of mechanical ventilation, sepsis, the dysfunction of two or more organs, female sex, administration of steroids, and hypercapnia are known risk factors for developing neuromuscular dysfunction. Syndromes of weakness in critically ill patients are relatively common and likely polymorphic in origin. In a retrospective study of 92 critically ill patients with clinically diagnosed weakness, electromyographic studies indicated that acute myopathy (critical illness myopathy [CIM]) is three times as common as acute axonal neuropathy (critical illness neuropathy): 43% vs. 13%, respectively.379 The additional health care cost of a single case of persistent weakness was estimated at approximately $67,000.380 Conditions to consider when making a differential diagnosis of neuromuscular weakness in the ICU are listed in Box 24.3.

Critical Illness Myopathy ​

Lacomis and colleagues suggested using the term CIM,381 instead of the current terms used in the literature, such as acute quadriplegic myopathy,382 acute (necrotizing) myopathy of intensive care, thick filament myopathy, acute corticosteroid myopathy, and critical care myopathy.

Initial published reports of CIM in the ICU focused on patients with status asthmaticus383 who typically had been treated with corticosteroids and nondepolarizing NMBDs. Nevertheless, myopathy has also been documented in asthmatic patients and in patients with chronic lung disease without paralysis who received corticosteroids,384 as well

as in critically ill patients with sepsis who received neither corticosteroids nor nondepolarizing NMBDs.385 The primary cause of this condition is a loss of myosin in myocytes with subsequent loss of contractile capacity. Animal studies also reveal that the number of cytosolic corticosteroid receptors is increased in immobilized muscles relative to that in contralateral controls.386 It seems—at least in some patients—that prolonged immobility may be the key risk factor for myopathy in corticosteroid- treated patients,387 and selective muscle atrophy is a result of changes in glucocorticoid sensitivity.386

Sepsis, immobility, and the catabolism associated with negative nitrogen balance may also result in myopathy.16 Skeletal muscle hypoperfusion is noted in patients with severe sepsis despite normal or elevated whole blood oxygen delivery.388 Antibodies to nAChRs were demonstrated in a rodent model of sepsis.389 Thus myasthenia- like syndrome is also seen in critically ill patients. Evidence for local immune activation by cytokine expression in the skeletal muscle was reported in patients with CIM.390

The major feature of CIM is flaccid weakness that tends to be diffuse and sometimes also includes the facial muscles and diaphragm.381 The clinical features of CIM overlap with those of critical illness polyneuropathy (CIP) and prolonged neuromuscular block.381 Electrophysiologic studies and increases in serum creatine kinase concentrations may differentiate neuropathy from myopathy.381 Lacomis and colleagues stated, “muscle biopsy should be considered if another myopathic process such as an inflammatory myopathy is suspected or if the histologic findings would affect management.”381

Critical Illness Polyneuropathy ​

Critical illness polyneuropathy (CIP) is the polyneuropathy seen in critically ill patients. It affects both sensory and motor nerves and occurs in 50% to 70% of patients with multisystem organ failure and systemic inflammatory response syndrome.391 Investigators have postulated that systemic inflammatory response syndrome contributes to CIP by releasing cytokines and free radicals that damage the microcirculation of the central and peripheral nervous systems.390 Dysregulation of the microcirculation may render the peripheral nervous system susceptible to injury.

Although no pharmacologic treatment for muscle weakness syndromes in the critically ill patient currently exists, increasing evidence indicates the positive effects of early physical rehabilitation during ICU stay. Previously, intensive insulin therapy during critical illness was found to decrease the risk of CIP, and the maintenance of blood glucose at or below 110 mg/mL in critically ill patients may reduce the risk of CIP.

The outcomes from CIM and CIP appear to be similar. The reported mortality rate of patients with CIP syndrome is approximately 35%. In one study, 100% (13 of 13) of the patients who survived had abnormal clinical or neurophysiologic findings 1 to 2 years after the onset of the CIP syndrome, and quality of life was markedly impaired in all patients.392

Clinical Implications ​

Nondepolarizing NMBDs are the most common chemical agents producing immobilization and inducing a

denervation- like state. In such circumstances, besides the mature or junctional nAChR formed of two α and one each of β, ε, and δ subunits, two other isoforms, the immature AChR or γAChR and the neuronal α7AChR, are expressed in the muscle. The immature AChR is also referred to as extrajunctional because it is expressed mostly in the extrajunctional part of the muscle. Upregulation of nAChRs was noted in the muscles of deceased critically ill adults who had received long- term infusions of vecuronium.393 Upregulation refers to changes in the number of available receptors, but these changes usually do not involve a change in isoform configuration. These three types of receptors can coexist in the muscle.

Should Succinylcholine Be Used in Patients in the Intensive Care Unit? ​

It is likely that upregulation of nAChRs induced by immobilization and by prolonged administration of nondepolarizing NMBDs contributes to (1) the higher incidence of cardiac arrest associated with the use of succinylcholine in ICU patients393 and (2) increased requirements for nondepolarizing NMBDs in ICU patients.394 Even more important, succinylcholine more easily depolarizes immature nAChRs that may induce profound outward K+ flux, with subsequent hyperkalemia. Moreover, the α7AChR can also be depolarized by succinylcholine, thus contributing to the K+ efflux from the cell to the extracellular space. Therefore, succinylcholine should be avoided in ICU patients in whom total body immobilization exceeds 24 hours.16

Should Nondepolarizing Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs Be Used in Patients in the Intensive Care Unit? ​

NMBD- associated persistent weakness appears to be a distinct pathologic entity and is not simply a manifestation of weakness syndromes in critically ill patients. In a prospective study, there was a 70% incidence of persistent weakness in ICU patients who received NMBDs for more than 2 days, compared with a 0% incidence in similar ICU patients who received no NMBD.395 This is compelling evidence for the effects of nondepolarizing NMBDs in this complication.

Long- term weakness has been described after nondepolarizing NMBDs.177,396,397 Approximately 20% of patients who received NMBDs for more than 6 days,396 15% to 40% of asthmatic patients who also received high- dose steroids,383 and 50% of patients with renal failure who received vecuronium developed prolonged weakness.177 Clinically, it appears that prolonged recovery from neuromuscular block occurs more frequently when steroidal NMBDs are used.177,396

However, prolonged weakness was also noted after the use of atracurium in ICU patients.397 Furthermore, the use of atracurium has raised concerns about its metabolite, laudanosine. Laudanosine, also detected in the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) of ICU patients given atracurium,398 is an analeptic and can trigger seizures in animals.399 The toxic dose in humans is not known, but case reports have noted patients having seizures while receiving atracurium, and laudanosine has not been ruled out as a cause of these seizures.400–402 Evidence also indicates that laudanosine can activate neuronal nicotinic receptors.403 Cisatracurium is a single isomer of atracurium, and because it is four to five

times more potent than atracurium, it is given in smaller total doses. Therefore, the risk of laudanosine- related adverse effects is minimal.404

Nondepolarizing NMBDs are polar molecules and do not readily cross the blood–brain barrier, but vecuronium and its long- acting active metabolite, 3- desacetylvecuronium, have been detected in the CSF of patients in the ICU. The CNS effects of NMBDs and their metabolites in humans have not been well studied, but in rats, atracurium, pancuronium, and vecuronium injected into the CSF caused dose- related cerebral excitation culminating in seizures.399 Cerebral excitation with consequent increased cerebral oxygen demand is undesirable in ICU patients at risk of cerebral ischemia. Investigators have also suggested that nondepolarizing NMBDs can gain access to nerves during systemic inflammatory response syndrome, thus resulting in direct neurotoxicity.399

When nondepolarizing NMBDs are necessary, the use of an objective neuromuscular monitor is recommended, and periodic return of muscle function should be allowed. Adjusting the dosage of NMBDs by peripheral nerve stimulation versus standard “clinical dosing” in critically ill patients reduces drug requirements, produces faster recovery of neuromuscular function, and is cost- effective.405 Daily interruption of sedative- drug infusions decreases the duration of mechanical ventilation and the length of stay in the ICU.406 The effect of such an approach on the weakness syndromes in the ICU patient is unknown. When nondepolarizing NMBDs are used, the guidelines in Box 24.4 may help minimize the incidence of complications. As stated in the clinical practice guidelines for sustained neuromuscular block in the adult critically ill patient,373 “ Independent of the reasons for using neuromuscular blockers, we emphasize that all other modalities to improve the clinical situation must be tried, using neuromuscular blockers only as a last resort .” To this admonition, we would add a plea that clinicians use objective monitors, whenever possible, to guide NMBD administration and assess readiness for tracheal extubation, whether in the operating room or ICU settings. The unquestionable benefits of quantitative neuromuscular monitoring are described in Chapter 39.

Reversal (Antagonism) of Neuromuscular Block ​

The importance of pharmacologic reversal of neuromuscular block was suggested in 1945, when the use of neostigmine or physostigmine to antagonize curare was recommended whenever neuromuscular blocking drugs were used in the operating room.407 The first large case series examining the use of curare was published by Cecil Gray in 1946.408 Notably, no postoperative complications directly attributable to d - tubocurarine were noted, and physostigmine was administered to only two patients in their series of 1049 surgical cases.408 However, in a later review article (1959) from the same anesthesia department, the authors concluded that “it is safer to always use neostigmine when nondepolarizing relaxants have been administered.”407

Although significant differences in opinion still existed in the United States and Europe regarding management

Current Antagonism Practice ​

A large- scale, comprehensive survey of neuromuscular management practices in the United States and Europe was conducted to better understand attitudes about doses of NMBDs, monitoring, and pharmacologic reversal.411 Only 18% of European respondents and 34.2% of respondents from the United States “always” administered an anticholinesterase drug when a nondepolarizing relaxant was used. The findings from surveys suggest that there was little agreement about best practices related to antagonism of neuromuscular block and that most clinicians do not monitor or antagonize a neuromuscular block in the operating room. A 2021 survey of European anesthesiologists reported a significant increase in the availability of quantitative monitors in many European countries; 61.4% of respondents reported that 1 quantitative monitor was available per operating room.412 Despite the clinically relevant information, the survey had several important limitations. First, the response rate was only 9.3% and there is a potential for significant response bias with anesthesiologists who were more interested in monitoring being more likely to respond. Second, there was significant variation in response rates between the 37 surveyed countries; more than 55% of total responses were from three countries, namely France, Belgium, and Switzerland. Third, it is not clear how often quantitative monitors were used. Thus, this survey may not reflect actual current practice.

Surprisingly, most anesthesiologists report not to have witnessed obvious adverse events directly attributable to incomplete recovery from neuromuscular block.411 In the following sections, the definitions, incidence, and clinical implications of residual neuromuscular block are reviewed. Recent perioperative guidelines from several national and international organizations have provided detailed recommendations on safe administration and reversal of neuromuscular blocking drugs in clinical practice.

RESIDUAL NEUROMUSCULAR BLOCK ​

Assessment of Residual Neuromuscular Block ​

To optimize patient safety, tracheal extubation in the operating room should not occur until recovery from neuromuscular block is complete as indicated by a train- of- four (TOF) ratio (the ratio of the fourth response, T4, to the first response, T1 – also known as the T4/T1 ratio) greater than 0.9 by electromyography (EMG) and mechanomyography (MMG) or TOF ratio >1.0 by acceleromyography (AMG). It is impossible to detect residual paralysis when using clinical tests or qualitative (subjective) assessment of TOF stimulation. Therefore, confirmation of recovery of the TOF ratio above 0.9 measured at the adductor pollicis muscle, preferably by EMG, is required before tracheal extubation410,413 (see Chapter 39).

Quantitative Neuromuscular Monitoring. When using TOF stimulation delivered by a peripheral nerve stimulator (PNS) (Fig. 24.14), investigators have consistently reported that clinicians are unable to subjectively (visually or tactilely) detect fade when TOF ratios exceed 0.30 to 0.40,414–416 whereas quantitative monitors (Fig. 24.15) allow an accurate assessment of the degree of muscle weakness using peripheral nerve stimulation and precise measurement of the evoked muscle responses (TOF ratio; TOF count; posttetanic count). In a study comparing AMG with standard qualitative tests (tactile detection of fade to TOF stimulation, double- burst stimulation, 50- Hz tetanic stimulation, and 100- Hz tetanic stimulation), quantitative monitoring was the most accurate technique in detecting residual paralysis (Fig. 24.16).416 In addition, the use of quantitative monitoring in the operating room reduces the risk of residual neuromuscular block in the postanesthesia care unit (PACU)417–420 and decreases adverse respiratory events and symptoms of muscle weakness associated with incomplete neuromuscular recovery.419,420 In clinical practice, neuromuscular block can be monitored with either EMG or AMG to determine the intraoperative depth of neuromuscular block; whether full recovery of neuromuscular function has occurred prior to performing tracheal extubation; and to provide objective data to guide dosing of reversal drugs (antagonists) at the conclusion of surgery (see below).

Quantitative neuromuscular monitoring is the only method of determining whether full recovery of muscular function has occurred and whether pharmacologic reversal can be safely avoided, and to exclude the possibility of residual neuromuscular block. For a comprehensive description of neuromuscular monitoring, see Chapter 39.

Symptoms and Definitions of Residual Neuromuscular Block ​

Symptoms of residual block include blurry vision, diplopia, facial weakness, facial numbness, and general weakness.421,422 At TOF ratios less than 0.90, awake volunteers exhibit impaired pharyngeal function, airway obstruction, increased risk of aspiration of gastric contents, impaired hypoxic ventilatory control, and unpleasant symptoms of muscle weakness.138,142,421,423,424 In surgical patients, an association between TOF ratios less than 0.90 and adverse

Fig. 24.14 Example of a qualitative neuromuscular instrument (or more appropriately termed, a peripheral nerve stimulator, PNS) (MiniStim, Halyard Health, Roswell, GA). A peripheral nerve (in this case, the ulnar nerve) is stimulated, and the response to nerve stimulation is assessed subjectively (qualitatively) using either visual observation or tactile means (detecting movement of the thumb). Note that the negative (black) stimulating electrode is placed distally.

respiratory events and prolonged PACU length of stay has been observed.425,426

Although most patients with TOF ratios between 0.90 and 1.0 will have recovered satisfactory strength in most muscle groups, clinically relevant signs and symptoms of muscle weakness may persist, including weakness of the respiratory muscles.427 The most inclusive and precise definition of residual neuromuscular block should include not only objective and quantifiable monitoring data (a TOF ratio <0.90 demonstrated with normalized AMG or EMG), but also clinical suggestions of impaired neuromuscular recovery (swallowing impairment, inability to speak or perform a head lift, diplopia, and/or general weakness).

Incidence of Residual Neuromuscular Block ​

Over several decades, incomplete neuromuscular recovery has been a common postoperative event. Current largescale studies (150–640 subjects) have repeatedly demonstrated that approximately 31% to 50% of patients still have clinically significant residual neuromuscular block with adductor pollicis muscle TOF ratios less than 0.90 following surgery.428–430 A recent multicenter investigation enrolling 1571 patients from 32 centers documented that 58% of patients had TOF ratios less than 0.90 at the time of tracheal extubation, despite the use of neostigmine reversal in 78% of subjects.431 In a meta-analysis of data from 24 clinical trials, Naguib and colleagues calculated the incidence of residual block by NMBD type (long- vs. intermediate- acting) according to a TOF ratio of >0.7 and >0.9.432 The pooled rate of residual block, defined as a TOF ratio less than 0.90, was 41% when studies using intermediate- acting NMBDs were analyzed (Table 24.14). Adverse Effects of Residual Block ​

Many investigations have demonstrated that approximately one-half of surgical patients will be admitted to the PACU with TOF ratios less than 0.90, as measured with AMG or EMG.432 Similar results of 36%–50% incidence of residual neuromuscular block in non- postoperative ICU patients also have been reported in practices where quantitative monitors are not used.437 Yet, even minimal levels of neuromuscular block may have clinical consequences.

The following section reviews the effects of residual block in both awake volunteer studies and in postoperative surgical patients.

Awake Volunteer Studies. Surgical patients receive a variety of anesthetics in the perioperative period, which complicates an assessment of the effect of residual neuromuscular block on clinical outcomes. Conducting awake, unmedicated volunteer trials allows investigators to more precisely quantify the impact of NMBDs and various degrees of neuromuscular block on physiologic systems in the absence of anesthetics.

Investigations have revealed that pharyngeal and respiratory function is impaired at TOF ratios as high as 0.90 to 1.0.438 Return of pharyngeal muscle function is essential for airway control following tracheal extubation. At adductor pollicis muscle–measured TOF ratios less than 0.90, pharyngeal dysfunction was observed in 17% to 28% of young adult volunteers (Fig. 24.18),138 increasing more than twofold in patients older than 60 years; this was associated with reduced upper esophageal sphincter resting

tone, misdirected swallowing, and aspiration (laryngeal penetration) of oral contrast material.138,423,439 A series of investigations examined the effect of residual paresis on respiratory muscle function in awake volunteers. At a minimal level of residual block (TOF ratio of approximately 0.80), the authors observed impaired inspiratory air flow and upper airway obstruction,424 a marked decrease in upper airway volumes and upper airway dilator muscle function,440 and increased upper airway closing pressure and collapsibility (Fig. 24.19).441 In addition, evidence from human studies of respiratory control suggest that residual neuromuscular block inhibits hypoxic ventilatory control while leaving the ventilatory control during hypercapnia unaffected.442 In human volunteers, the hypoxic ventilatory response was attenuated by 30% after administration of atracurium, vecuronium, or pancuronium at an adductor pollicis TOF ratio of 0.70, returning to normal after spontaneous recovery to a TOF ratio of greater than 0.90 (Fig. 24.20).443 Other investigators have recently shown that despite recovery to a TOF of 0.9, impairment of the peripheral reflex may persist in some volunteers.444

An increase in ventilatory drive during hypoxia is primarily mediated by afferent input from peripheral chemoreceptors in the carotid bodies located bilaterally at the carotid artery bifurcation, whereas ventilatory regulation during hypercapnia is mediated via CO2 interaction with brainstem chemoreceptors. In experimental animals, the firing frequencies of carotid body chemoreceptors are almost abolished by the administration of a nondepolarizing NMBD, via block of cholinergic neuronal subtype receptors within the carotid body oxygen signaling pathway.445

Awake volunteer studies have also revealed that unpleasant symptoms of muscle weakness are present in subjects with small degrees of residual neuromuscular block (TOF

Postoperative Surgical Patients. Similar adverse events have been observed in postoperative surgical patients who had TOF ratios less than 0.90 measured in the PACU. Incomplete neuromuscular recovery is a risk factor for hypoxemic events, airway obstruction, unpleasant symptoms of muscle weakness, delayed PACU length of stay, and pulmonary complications during the early postoperative period.419,447–449

A large case–control investigation was performed of all patients undergoing anesthesia over a 3- year period ( n = 869,483) in The Netherlands; it assessed the impact of anesthetic management characteristics on the risk of coma or death within 24 hours of surgery.450 Reversal of the effects of NMBDs was associated with a significant reduction (odds ratio, 0.10; 95% confidence interval [CI], 0.03–0.31) in the risk of these complications. Two studies published in 2016 and 2017 examined the association between failure to reverse neuromuscular block and postoperative pneumonia.141,451 In an investigation examining 13,100 surgical patients, Bulka and associates observed that the risk of postoperative pneumonia was 2.26 times higher in patients who did not receive neostigmine reversal.141 Similarly, a retrospective study of 11,355 noncardiac patients revealed that the risk of respiratory complications (failure to wean

from the ventilator, reintubation, or pneumonia) was significantly higher (odds ratio 1.75) in patients who received an NMBD without neostigmine antagonism compared to those given neostigmine.141

To address a major limitation of previous outcome studies (the lack of quantification of residual paralysis), recent studies have quantified TOF ratios in the PACU and documented a relationship between presence of residual block and adverse outcomes. Several clinical investigations have documented an association between postoperative residual block and adverse respiratory events. In an observational study, patients with TOF ratios less than 0.70 in the PACU had a higher incidence of hypoxemia (60%) compared with patients with TOF ratios 0.70 or greater (10%, P <.05).452 Another small study in orthopedic surgical patients who were randomized to receive either pancuronium or rocuronium revealed that patients with TOF ratios less than 0.90 on arrival to the PACU were more likely to develop postoperative hypoxemia (24 of 39 patients, 62%) than those with TOF ratios greater than 0.90 (7 of 30 patients, 23%; P = .003).453 Murphy and associates conducted a case–control study examining the incidence and severity of residual block in patients who developed critical respiratory events in the PACU.425 Seventy- four percent of patients in the group with critical respiratory events had TOF ratios less than 0.70, compared with 0% in the control group (matched for age, sex, and surgical procedure). Because the two cohorts did not differ in any perioperative characteristics except for neuromuscular recovery, these findings suggest that unrecognized residual paralysis is an important contributing factor to postoperative adverse respiratory events. Another investigation by this same group examined the effect of AMG monitoring on postoperative respiratory events.419 Few patients randomized to AMG monitoring had postoperative TOF ratios less than 0.90, and a lower incidence of early hypoxemia and airway obstruction were observed in this group (compared with patients randomized to standard qualitative monitoring).

A study of 114 patients randomized to neostigmine reversal or placebo (saline) documented a significantly higher incidence of both postoperative residual block and

hypoxemia in the placebo group.454 Residual block in the PACU may also result in pulmonary complications within the first postoperative week. Berg and colleagues randomized 691 patients to receive pancuronium, atracurium, or vecuronium.139 TOF ratios were quantified in the PACU, and patients were followed for 6 days for pulmonary complications. In the pancuronium group, significantly more patients with TOF ratios less than 0.70 developed a pulmonary complication (16.9%) compared with patients with TOF 0.70 or greater (4.8%). Notably, the study also demonstrated a continuously increased risk for postoperative pulmonary complications with increasing age. This is a finding of significant clinical relevance for older adult patients, who are a growing surgical patient population. Norton and colleagues assessed recovery characteristics in 202 consecutive patients arriving in the PACU. Only thirty percent of patients had TOF ratios greater than 0.9; patients with residual block had a significantly higher incidence of critical respiratory events, airway obstruction, hypoxemia, and respiratory failure.455 An observational study enrolling 150 patients aged 18 to 50, and 150 patients more than 70 years old, assessed the association between incomplete neuromuscular recovery (TOF <0.9) and adverse events from the time of tracheal extubation until hospital discharge.456 Elderly subjects had a higher risk of residual block (58% vs. 30%), and those elderly with TOF ratios less than 0.9 had a significantly higher incidence of airway obstruction episodes and hypoxemic events, as well as signs and symptoms of muscle weakness.456 A multicenter study from Spain enrolling 763 patients from 26 centers reported that 27% of patients had TOF ratios less than 0.9, and that these patients had a higher incidence of adverse respiratory events (odds ratio 2.57) and an increased risk of reintubation.448

The residual effects of NMBDs on postoperative muscle strength may impair clinical recovery and prolong PACU discharge times. Butterly et al. measured TOF ratios in 246 consecutive patients on arrival to the PACU.426 The PACU length of stay was significantly longer in patients with TOF ratios less than 0.90 (323 minutes) compared with patients with adequate recovery of neuromuscular function (243 minutes). Multiple regression analysis revealed that only

In a large European prospective observational trail (POPULAR) that included more than 21,000 patients, it was shown that NMBA use without adequate quantitative monitoring or appropriate neuromuscular antagonism could lead to severe pulmonary events.457 Interestingly, in their post hoc analysis, the authors were able to demonstrate that postoperative pulmonary complications were reduced in patients who attained a train- offour ratio above 0.95 before tracheal extubation.458

In conclusion, epidemiologic outcome investigations have suggested an association between incomplete neuromuscular recovery and major morbidity and mortality. Prospective clinical trials have revealed that patients with TOF ratios less than 0.90 in the PACU are at increased risk for hypoxemic events, airway obstruction, postoperative pulmonary complications, symptoms of muscle weakness, and prolonged PACU admission times. Therefore, appropriate management of reversal of neuromuscular block and assessment of recovery from neuromuscular block by quantitative monitoring are two essential clinical components to optimize patient outcomes. A recent narrative review of the literature has addressed the potential benefits of monitoring in the perioperative setting.459

Drugs Used to Antagonize (Reverse) Neuromuscular Block ​

Reversal of neuromuscular block is theoretically possible by four principal mechanisms: (1) an increase in presynaptic release of acetylcholine; (2) a decrease in enzymatic metabolism of acetylcholine by cholinesterase, thereby increasing receptor binding competition; (3) a decrease in the concentration of the NMBD at the effect site, allowing normal function of the postsynaptic receptors; and (4) inhibition of the skeletal muscle- specific chloride ion channel (ClC- 1).460

ANTICHOLINESTERASE REVERSAL OF NEUROMUSCULAR BLOCK ​

Nondepolarizing NMBDs inhibit neuromuscular transmission primarily by competitively antagonizing or blocking the effect of acetylcholine at the postjunctional nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (nAChR). If larger concentrations of acetylcholine are present at the neuromuscular junction, acetylcholine will be more likely to attach to the postsynaptic receptor and facilitate neuromuscular transmission and muscle contraction. Conversely, if larger concentrations of a nondepolarizing NMBD are present at the neuromuscular junction, binding to α subunits of the receptor will preferentially occur, preventing central pore opening and muscle depolarization. A more detailed description of the neuromuscular junction is provided in Chapter 11.

One mechanism of antagonizing the effects of NMBDs is by increasing the concentration of acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction. This can be accomplished using an inhibitor of cholinesterase, the enzyme that breaks down acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction

(acetylcholinesterase). Over the six decades prior to the approval of sugammadex, anticholinesterases had been the only drugs used clinically to antagonize neuromuscular block. Three anticholinesterase drugs are used in clinical practice: neostigmine, edrophonium, and pyridostigmine. Neostigmine is the most commonly administered drug and will be discussed in greater detail; edrophonium has not been available in the United States since 2018; pyridostigmine is used clinically in Asia, but rarely in the United States. Anticholinesterases remain the only drugs available for pharmacologic antagonism of benzylisoquinolinium NMBDs.

Mechanism of Action of Anticholinesterases ​

Acetylcholine is the primary neurotransmitter that is synthesized, stored, and released by exocytosis at the distal motor nerve terminal. Acetylcholinesterase is the enzyme responsible for the control of neurotransmission at the neuromuscular junction by hydrolyzing acetylcholine. Rapid hydrolysis of acetylcholine removes excess neurotransmitter from the synapse, preventing overstimulation and tetanic excitation of the postsynaptic muscle. Nearly half of the acetylcholine molecules released from the presynaptic nerve membrane are hydrolyzed by acetylcholinesterase before reaching the postsynaptic nAChR.16 The action of acetylcholinesterase is extremely rapid; acetylcholine molecules are hydrolyzed in approximately 80 to 100 μs (microseconds). Acetylcholinesterase is concentrated at the neuromuscular junction, and there are approximately 10 enzyme- binding sites for each molecule of acetylcholine released.461 However, lower concentrations of acetylcholinesterase are present along the length of the muscle fiber. Each molecule of acetylcholinesterase has an active surface with two important binding sites, an anionic site and an esteratic site. The negatively charged anionic site on the acetylcholinesterase molecule is responsible for electrostatically binding the positively charged quaternary nitrogen group on the acetylcholine molecule. The esteratic site forms covalent bonds with the carbamate group at the opposite end of the acetylcholine molecule and is responsible for the hydrolytic process (Fig. 24.21).461 In addition, a secondary or peripheral anionic site has been proposed. Binding of ligands to the peripheral anionic site results in inactivation of the enzyme.

The anticholinesterase drugs used by anesthesiologists interact with the anionic and esteratic sites of acetylcholinesterase. These drugs are characterized as either prosthetic inhibitors (edrophonium) or oxydiaphoretic (acid- transferring) inhibitors (neostigmine, pyridostigmine) of the enzyme. Edrophonium rapidly binds to the anionic site via electrostatic forces and to the esteratic site by hydrogen bonding.16,461 Rapid binding may account for the short onset of action of edrophonium in clinical practice. During the time edrophonium is bound, the enzyme is inactive and edrophonium is not metabolized. However, the interaction between edrophonium and acetylcholinesterase is weak and short- lived. The dissociation half- life of this interaction is approximately 20 to 30 seconds, and the interaction between drug and enzyme is competitive and reversible. Because the nature of the binding (non- covalent) is relatively brief, the efficacy of edrophonium in reversing

neuromuscular block may be limited. Neostigmine and pyridostigmine are oxydiaphoretic inhibitors of acetylcholinesterase, which also bind to the anionic site. In addition, these drugs transfer a carbamate group to acetylcholinesterase, creating a covalent bond at the esteratic site.16,461 This reaction results in an inactivation of the enzyme, as well as the hydrolysis of the drug. The stronger interaction between neostigmine and enzyme results in a dissociation half- life of approximately 7 minutes.461 These interactions at the molecular level likely have little impact on the duration of action in clinical practice. Duration of clinical effect is primarily determined by removal of anticholinesterase from the plasma.462

The administration of anticholinesterases has also been reported to produce presynaptic effects.462 Laboratory investigations have demonstrated that these prejunctional effects may facilitate neuromuscular transmission. Anticholinesterases produce a reversible increase in the duration of the action potential and refractory period of the nerve terminal. Because the quantity of acetylcholine released is a function of the extent and duration of the depolarization of the terminal membrane, the period of acetylcholine release in response to nerve stimulation may be increased by anticholinesterase agents.462 Excessive release of acetylcholine, coupled with decreased hydrolysis due to acetylcholinesterase inhibition, results in prolonged end- plate potentials and repetitive firing of muscle fibers. These prejunctional effects appear to account for the observations that spontaneous contractions of muscles can occur when anticholinesterases are given in the absence of NMBDs.462

As concentrations of acetylcholine increase, some of the neurotransmitter diffuses away from the neuromuscular junction, while additional acetylcholine undergoes reuptake into motor nerve terminals. As the processes of diffusion and reuptake reach equilibrium with augmented release by enzyme inhibition, a “peak” level at the neuromuscular junction is reached.461 Once the acetylcholinesterase enzyme is maximally inhibited by an anticholinesterase agent and peak concentrations of acetylcholine are present, the administration of additional drug will not further increase acetylcholine levels or enhance recovery of neuromuscular block. This is referred to as the “ceiling effect” of anticholinesterases and is an important limitation of all clinically used anticholinesterase agents.

Two separate processes contribute to recovery of neuromuscular function following anticholinesterase administration. The first is the inhibition of acetylcholinesterase at the neuromuscular junction produced by neostigmine, pyridostigmine, or edrophonium. The second is the spontaneous process of decrease in the concentration of the NMBD at the neuromuscular junction over time due to redistribution, metabolism, and elimination. Because of the ceiling effect of anticholinesterases, neuromuscular block cannot be adequately reversed if high concentrations of NMBDs are present at the neuromuscular junction. Consequently, if antagonism to TOF ratio ≥0.90 is not attained when acetylcholinesterase is maximally inhibited, this means that the concentration of NMBD is too high (i.e., the block is too deep) for efficacious antagonism by cholinesterase inhibitors. Obviously, earlier administration, during an even higher concentration of NMBD, would not improve efficacy.

Pharmacokinetic and Pharmacodynamic ​

Properties of Anticholinesterases ​

Many clinical studies have examined the pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic characteristics of neostigmine, pyridostigmine, and edrophonium.

The pharmacokinetic profiles of neostigmine, pyridostigmine, and edrophonium are presented in Table 24.15. Most studies have used a two- compartment model to establish pharmacokinetic characteristics of each agent. Following a bolus administration, plasma concentrations peak rapidly and decline significantly within the first 5 to 10 minutes. This is followed by a slower decline in plasma concentrations due to the elimination phase.462 In general, the pharmacokinetic profiles of all three anticholinesterases are similar. Early studies suggested that the duration of edrophonium was too short for clinical use. However, studies using larger doses (0.5 or 1.0 mg/kg) demonstrated that the elimination half- life of edrophonium was not significantly different from that of neostigmine or pyridostigmine and that edrophonium could produce prompt and sustained reversal of neuromuscular block.463,464 The longer elimination half- life of pyridostigmine likely accounts for the longer duration of action compared with the other anticholinesterase drugs.465

The pharmacokinetics of anticholinesterases can be influenced by renal function, age, and body temperature. The elimination half- life of all three agents is altered by the presence of renal insufficiency or failure (see Table 24.15). Renal excretion accounts for approximately 50% of plasma clearance of neostigmine; elimination half- life is significantly prolonged and serum clearance decreased in anephric patients.466 Similarly, renal function accounts for 70% to 75% of serum clearance of pyridostigmine and edrophonium.465,467 The reduced plasma clearance of the anticholinesterases in renal failure patients provides a “margin of safety” against the risk of postoperative “recurarization” (the effects of the NMBD persist longer than those of the reversal agent, resulting in a worsening of residual paresis). The pharmacokinetics of edrophonium have been examined in older adult (age >70 years) patients. When compared with a younger cohort, older adult patients exhibited a significant decrease in plasma clearance (5.9 ± 2 vs. 12.1 ± 4 mL/kg/min) and a prolonged elimination half- life (84.2 ± 17 vs. 56.6 ± 16 minutes).468 Mild hypothermia

(reduction in core temperature of 2°C) more than doubles the duration of action of intermediate- acting NMBDs.469 In a study of human volunteers cooled to 34.5°C, the central volume of distribution of neostigmine decreased by 38% and the onset time of maximal effect increased from 4.6 to 5.6 minutes.274 However, the clearance, maximal effect, and duration of action of neostigmine were not altered by a reduction in body temperature. Therefore, if hypothermia influences the degree of neuromuscular recovery, it is likely secondary to an effect on the pharmacology of NMBDs (not the anticholinesterase).

Onset of action is more rapid with edrophonium than with either neostigmine or pyridostigmine. When d - tubocurarine neuromuscular block was antagonized with approximately equipotent doses of the three clinically used anticholinesterases, the peak effect of antagonism was reached significantly faster with edrophonium (0.8–2.0 minutes) than with neostigmine (7–11 minutes) or pyridostigmine (12–16 minutes) (Fig. 24.22).463 Similar findings have been observed in patients receiving other long- and intermediate- acting NMBDs. When larger doses (0.5–1.0 mg/kg) of edrophonium are administered during moderate levels of neuromuscular block (10% recovery of single- twitch height after pancuronium or atracurium), the onset time of edrophonium was faster than neostigmine.470,471 During deeper levels of block (<10% recovery of single twitch), edrophonium 1.0 mg/ kg and neostigmine 0.04 mg/kg had similar onset times when vecuronium was used (and both were faster than edrophonium 0.5 mg/kg).472 When pancuronium was antagonized during deep block, edrophonium 1.0 mg/kg had a shorter onset time than neostigmine 0.04 mg/kg.472 These findings suggest that onset time of antagonism is influenced by the type and dose of anticholinesterase used, the choice of NMBD administered intraoperatively, and the depth of neuromuscular block at the time of antagonism.

The duration of action of anticholinesterases is determined not only by the pharmacokinetic properties of the drugs, but also by the concentration of NMBD present at the neuromuscular junction at the time of reversal. The neuromuscular block will naturally decrease over time because of redistribution, metabolism, and elimination of NMBDs. To accurately assess the durations of action of anticholinesterases during a stable neuromuscular block,

Factors Determining the Adequacy of Recovery Following Administration of Anticholinesterases ​

Depth of Neuromuscular Block at the Time of Reversal. The primary anesthetic management variable determining the effectiveness of anticholinesterase agents in completely antagonizing neuromuscular block at the end of surgery is the depth of neuromuscular block at the time of reversal. It has been stated that “antagonism of block by anticholinesterases should not be attempted until some evidence of spontaneous recovery is present.” This recommendation is no longer considered sufficient or appropriate, however, as most recent guidelines recommend that

anticholinesterases should only be administered if advanced spontaneous neuromuscular recovery (i.e., TOF ratio >0.2) has been attained.409,410

The time required to achieve a TOF ratio of 0.90 after anticholinesterase administration is significantly shorter when a higher TOF count (TOF count of 3 or 4) is present at the time of reversal. Two studies have examined the efficacy of antagonizing residual block at varying TOF counts. Kirkegaard and colleagues randomized patients receiving cisatracurium to reversal with neostigmine (0.07 mg/kg) at the reappearance of the first, second, third, and fourth tactile TOF response (TOF count 1–4).474 The median (range) time required to achieve a TOF ratio of 0.90 was 22.2 (13.9–44.0) minutes when reversal was attempted at a TOF count of 1. However, even when four responses were present, the time needed to attain a TOF ratio of 0.90 was 16.5 (6.5–143.3) minutes (Table 24.16). Kim and associates performed a similar study in which patients receiving rocuronium were randomized to receive antagonism at the first through fourth tactile TOF responses.475 In those patients receiving sevoflurane for anesthetic maintenance, the median (range) time required to achieve a TOF ratio of 0.90 was 28.6 (8.8–75.8) minutes when reversed at a TOF count of 1 and 9.7 (5.1–26.4) minutes when antagonism was at a TOF count of 4. In both investigations, a large interindividual variability in reversal times was observed and the time to attain TOF ratio ≥0.9 was progressively more unpredictable with lower TOF counts (i.e., deeper block) at the time of reversal.474,475 The variability is likely a reflection of the individual response to the NMBD administered. Both studies demonstrated that it was not possible to reliably achieve full neuromuscular recovery (TOF ratio of >0.90) in most patients within 10 minutes of anticholinesterase administration. Moreover, most patients who received volatile anesthesia in these studies had a TOF ratio of 0.4–0.9 at 10 minutes after neostigmine administration; thus, using qualitative assessment (based on use of PNS), the most likely finding would be a TOF count of 4

without fade (“4 equal twitches”). In this situation, it is not possible to predict when an individual patient will attain a TOF ratio of at least 0.90 to allow safe tracheal extubation. Therefore, in the absence of quantitative neuromuscular monitoring, antagonism of moderate and shallow block with neostigmine is no longer recommended.409,410

When the neuromuscular block has recovered to the level of minimal block (TOF ratio 0.4 to 0.9, see Table 24.6), neostigmine antagonism becomes significantly more efficacious. Fuchs- Buder and colleagues investigated the dose–response relationship of neostigmine using AMGbased monitoring to guide neostigmine- based reversal (10, 20, or 30 mcg/kg) given at TOF ratios of either 0.40 or 0.60 (Fig. 24.24).476 Volatile agents were not used in this study and all patients attained a non- normalized TOF ratio of 0.90 within 10 minutes of receiving 20 mcg/kg of neostigmine. However, when using non- normalized AMG, attainment of TOF ratio 1.0 is considered the minimum acceptable threshold of recovery, and neostigmine 30 mcg/ kg was more effective than 20 mcg/kg in achieving this level of recovery. A follow- up study was conducted during inhalational volatile anesthesia with AMG- based monitoring to guide antagonism of neuromuscular block at a TOF ratio of 0.6.477 The authors found that 10 mcg/kg was as effective as 20 and 30 mcg/kg, and all patients attained TOF ratio of 1.0 ± 0.05 within 10 minutes. No decrease in TOF ratio (re- paralysis) was observed in any patient after any of the three neostigmine doses given. These findings demonstrate that small doses of neostigmine can be safely used if both the pre- reversal depth of block and neuromuscular recovery are measured with quantitative monitors. If muscle function is assessed manually with a peripheral nerve stimulator and no fade is detected manually after TOF stimulation, the TOF ratio is likely at least 0.40, but may be as high as 0.90 or 1.0. In the setting of

full neuromuscular recovery, neostigmine administration may rarely produce paradoxical muscle weakness (see later). This potential risk should be considered if neostigmine is used to reverse minimal block based on the results of qualitative neuromuscular monitoring and is an important reason not to use higher doses of neostigmine than are required. The authors chose the pre- reversal thresholds of TOF ratio 0.4 and 0.6, respectively, because it was considered possible for clinicians to determine these same thresholds by qualitative (tactile) assessment; this would be consistent with previous reports suggesting that “loss of subjective fade” in the TOF and DBS responses occurs at approximate TOF ratios of 0.4 and 0.6, respectively. However, the lack of precision when using qualitative assessment to determine when spontaneous recovery has progressed to TOF ratio 0.4 is a significant limitation when guiding efficacious antagonism by neostigmine. Fig. 24.25 shows the results from two recent trials,478,479 in which neostigmine was used consistent with recently adopted guidelines409,410 : i.e., selectively for antagonism of minimal block. When minimal block was determined by qualitative (subjective) assessment and an antagonist was administered only when the clinician did not tactilely (subjectively) appreciate any fade, many patients did not have minimal block correctly identified. In contrast, when minimal block was determined quantitatively, all patients had minimal block identified correctly. Most importantly, the correct identification of minimal block and selective use of neostigmine only for patients with minimal block were associated with the prevention of residual block in a mixed surgical cohort of 189 patients.479

When quantitative monitoring is used, neostigmine may be considered for a depth of block deeper than minimal (TOF ratio 0.40 to 0.90), with the understanding that deeper blocks will require more time to attain a TOF ratio ≥0.90. A reasonable justification for administering neostigmine for antagonism of a quantitatively determined shallow block (TOF ratio <0.4) is that some patients may have a more rapid recovery from this deeper level of block than spontaneous recovery may require.

Time Interval Between Anticholinesterase Administration and Tracheal Extubation. Studies of time to peak effect of neostigmine have reported times between 4.6 and 11.1 minutes.252,274,473,480 Given that time to peak effect can require up to 11 minutes implies that the full effect of neostigmine antagonism cannot be assessed in the first minutes following injection. In many clinical situations, anticholinesterases have been given at the conclusion of surgical closure, with tracheal extubation performed shortly thereafter. A survey of anesthesiologists from Europe and the United States revealed that approximately one-half of respondents allowed only 5 minutes or less between anticholinesterase administration and tracheal extubation.411 In a study of 120 surgical patients who received rocuronium for tracheal intubation and maintenance of muscle relaxation, TOF ratios were quantified at the time of tracheal extubation when clinicians had determined that full recovery of neuromuscular function had occurred using clinical criteria and qualitative neuromuscular assessment (Fig. 24.26).435 Mean TOF ratios of 0.67 were observed immediately before extubation, with 88% of patients exhibiting TOF less than 0.90. Of note, the median TOF count at reversal was 4, and the average time interval between neostigmine administration and tracheal extubation was 8 minutes. The frequent incidence of residual block reported in multiple studies is likely attributable to the fact that anticholinesterases are administered without awaiting adequate pre- reversal spontaneous recovery, and not allowing at least 10 minutes to elapse between neostigmine and tracheal extubation. A recent meta- analysis has reported the proportion of patients with residual neuromuscular block up to 60 minutes after antagonism of moderate block (Fig. 24.27) and deep block (Fig. 24.28) with neostigmine or sugammadex.481

Type of Neuromuscular Blocking Drug Used Intraoperatively (Long- Acting Versus Intermediate- Acting). Several clinical investigations have examined the incidence of residual block in the PACU in patients receiving either intermediate- or long- acting NMBDs. These studies have consistently demonstrated that fewer patients who received intermediate- acting NMBD had residual block compared with those receiving long- acting agents. A meta-analysis of 24 clinical trials examined the pooled estimated incidence of residual block (defined as a TOF ratio <0.90) by neuromuscular blocking drugs type.432 The risk of residual block was significantly less in patients given intermediate- acting NMBDs (41%) versus long- acting NMBDs (72%). In conclusion, the probability of incomplete neuromuscular recovery in the early postoperative period is decreased when shorteracting NMBDs are used intraoperatively.

Type and Dose of Anticholinesterase. When antagonizing shallow or minimal block with neostigmine, larger doses of anticholinesterases result in more rapid and complete reversal of neuromuscular block than smaller doses. However, that the peak effect of neostigmine antagonism may not occur until 11 minutes after administration implies that, clinically, the full effect cannot be assessed in the first few minutes after administration. It also implies that if reversal is adequate within those first few minutes, then a more than adequate dose has been administered. It

Patients with train-of-four (TOF) ratio of zero include those with TOF count 0, 1, 2, and 3. The assumption that the threshold of TOF ratio 0.4 can be qualitatively (subjectively) identified by determining whether there is fade or no fade is unreliable. When using only peripheral nerve stimulator (PNS), anesthesia providers assessed six patients as having no fade, and therefore administered neostigmine, although their TOF ratios were <0.4. Among these six patients were three of the five cases of postoperative residual neuromuscular block in the entire study cohort. Providers assessed 18 patients as having fade although the TOF ratios were ≥0.4 and therefore administered sugammadex to these patients. In contrast, when anesthesia providers used quantitative monitoring, the threshold of TOF ratio 0.4 was correctly identified for all patients, and there were no cases of postoperative residual neuromuscular block. (From Thilen SR, Liang T, Kruse TN, et al. Evaluation of a protocol for the management of maintenance and reversal of rocuronium block using neostigmine or sugammadex. Anesth Analg . 2023;136(6):1143–1153; and Thilen SR, Sherpa JR, James AM, et al. Management of muscle relaxation with rocuronium and reversal with neostigmine or sugammadex guided by quantitative neuromuscular monitoring. Anesth Analg. 2023, May 12.)

is assumed that the incidence and severity of any potential adverse effects will be less with lower doses. Therefore, the clinician needs to consider whether it is important to accomplish antagonism more rapidly but with an increased risk of side effects. Sugammadex and neostigmine reversal of minimal block (TOF ratio 0.5) were compared.482 The authors evaluated neostigmine 5, 8, 15, 25, and 40 mcg/kg and reported that all patients who received at least 8 mcg/ kg attained an EMG- measured TOF ratio >0.9 in less than 9 minutes. By increasing the neostigmine dose, antagonism could be accelerated, and the authors concluded that a dose

of 34 mcg/kg was as effective as sugammadex (0.22 mg/ kg). It is probably rare that this more rapid recovery would be a clinical priority and using the lowest effective dose of neostigmine and glycopyrrolate may be more desirable. The relationship between dose and speed of recovery remains true until the maximal dose of anticholinesterase has been administered. At this point, acetylcholinesterase is maximally inhibited, and additional amounts of anticholinesterase will result in no further antagonism. Maximal effective doses of neostigmine have not been clearly defined, but may have been overestimated, and there is currently insufficient

evidence to support the contention that doses higher than 50 mcg/kg provide additional benefit.

(15 mcg/kg) compared with adults (23 mcg/kg).483 The times to peak antagonism and duration of antagonism did not differ between infants, children, and adults. Pharmacokinetic modeling revealed that distribution half- lives and volumes were similar in all three cohorts, although elimination half- life was shorter in infants and children than in adults. As in adults, the depth of neuromuscular block at the time of antagonism was a primary factor determining adequacy of recovery.484,485 Spontaneous recovery from neuromuscular block is more rapid in children compared with adults.485 However, when neostigmine was administered at various levels of block, the times to achieve neuromuscular recovery were similar in children and adults (the times to reach a TOF ratio of 0.90 were reduced by 30%–40% compared with spontaneous recovery).485 Thus in the clinical setting, reversal of neuromuscular block does not appear to differ significantly between children and adults.

older adults. Physiologic changes occur during the aging process that result in alterations in the response of older patients to NMBDs. These changes include an increase in body fat, a decrease in total body water, and declines in cardiac, hepatic, and renal function. In addition, anatomic alterations occur at the neuromuscular junction in older adults, such as a decrease in the concentration of nAChRs at the motor end plate and a reduction in the release of acetylcholine from the preterminal axon. All these factors contribute to a prolongation of effect of most NMBDs in older

patients. In a study comparing older adults (age >70) to younger controls, plasma clearance of edrophonium was decreased and elimination half- life prolonged in the aged cohort. Despite higher plasma concentrations of edrophonium, however, duration of antagonism was not increased. In contrast, other investigators observed that the duration of action of both neostigmine and pyridostigmine was significantly longer in older adults (age >60) compared with younger subjects.486 These findings suggest that plasma concentrations and/or duration of action of both NMBDs and anticholinesterases (neostigmine and pyridostigmine) are prolonged in older patients, which should reduce the risk of recurrence of neuromuscular block (re- paralysis). The risk of postoperative residual block in older adults (age >70 years) who have received neostigmine is significantly higher than in a younger cohort receiving similar doses of this drug (ages 18–50, 58% vs. 30%, respectively).456

Type of Anesthesia. Volatile anesthetics potentiate the action of nondepolarizing NMBDs when compared with intravenous anesthetics. Furthermore, volatile anesthetics interfere with the antagonism of neuromuscular block.487 Kim and colleagues randomized patients to receive a propofol or sevoflurane anesthetic (Table 24.17).475 The times required to achieve a TOF ratio of 0.70, 0.80, and 0.90 were significantly longer in patients given the sevofluranebased anesthetic compared with the propofol- based technique. Similar findings have been observed in patients

randomized to receive either isoflurane or propofol (neuromuscular recovery was delayed when a volatile anesthetic was used).487,488 These findings suggest that when the prereversal depth of block is deeper than minimal, the probability of achieving a TOF ratio greater than 0.90 within 10 to 15 minutes of anticholinesterase administration is increased if a total intravenous anesthetic technique is administered as opposed to a volatile anesthetic.

continuous infusion versus bolus administration of neuromuscular blocking drugs. Recovery from neuromuscular block may also be influenced by mode of NMBD administration. Jellish and colleagues examined recovery characteristics of rocuronium and cisatracurium when given as either a bolus or continuous infusion.488 The time required to reach a TOF ratio of 0.75 in the cisatracurium group was similar whether bolus or infusion techniques were used, whereas recovery was delayed when rocuronium was given as an infusion.488 The authors conclude that cisatracurium may be the agent of choice for prolonged procedures since its recovery is not affected by the duration of NMBD infusion.

Renal Function. As previously noted, renal excretion accounts for 50% to 75% of plasma clearance of neostigmine, pyridostigmine, and edrophonium. In anephric patients, elimination half- life of all three anticholinesterases is prolonged, and total plasma clearance of these agents is decreased (see Table 24.15). Similar changes in the

pharmacokinetic characteristics of nondepolarizing NMBDs have been noted in patients with renal failure. Therefore, management of anticholinesterase reversal should be similar in patients with normal and impaired renal function. Postoperative residual neuromuscular block in patients with renal failure is more likely secondary to improper titration of NMBDs intraoperatively rather than to inappropriate dosing of anticholinesterase agents.

Acid–Base Status of the Patient. The influence of metabolic status and respiratory acid–base balance on reversal of neuromuscular block has been investigated in the laboratory setting. Miller and associates noted that respiratory alkalosis and metabolic acidosis did not alter the dose of neostigmine needed to reverse a d - tubocurarine or pancuronium block. However, during respiratory acidosis and metabolic alkalosis, the dose of neostigmine needed to produce a comparable level of neuromuscular recovery was nearly twice as large.489,490 Although clinical studies have not been performed, the findings from laboratory investigations suggest that complete reversal of neuromuscular block may be difficult in the presence of respiratory acidosis and metabolic alkalosis. Clinicians should be aware of the risk of residual block in the setting of respiratory acidosis. Several anesthetics (opioids, benzodiazepines, volatile anesthetics) can potentially depress the ventilatory drive in the early postoperative period. This respiratory depression may result in respiratory acidosis, which limits the ability of anticholinesterases to antagonize neuromuscular block. The resultant residual block may further depress the respiratory muscle strength and ventilatory drive and increase the risk of adverse postoperative events.

their antagonists.409,410,413,491 Neostigmine is most effective when used for antagonism of minimal block. If quantitative monitors are not available and subjective evaluation using a peripheral nerve stimulator is necessary, then low doses of anticholinesterases (e.g., 20 mcg/kg of neostigmine; see later) can be considered when four responses are present with no discernible fade.

In many clinical settings quantitative neuromuscular monitoring is unfortunately not used and decisions relating to the administration of anticholinesterases are based on the time elapsed between the last dose of NMBD and the conclusion of surgery. Clinical studies do not support this practice. In a study of patients receiving a single intubating dose of vecuronium (0.1 mg/kg), 8.4% of patients had TOF ratios less than 0.80 even 4 hours after NMBD administration.492 Debaene and colleagues examined the incidence of residual block in a large cohort of patients who received a single intubating dose of vecuronium, rocuronium, or atracurium.429 Of the 239 patients who were tested 2 or more hours after administration of the NMBD, 37% had a TOF ratio less than 0.90 (Fig. 24.29). Murphy and associates administered a single 1 × ED95 dose of rocuronium (average dose 25 mg) to 120 patients.493 Despite an average case duration of 161 minutes, 21% of patients had not achieved a TOF ratio of 0.90 at the end of surgery. These investigations, as well as several pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic studies, demonstrate that the time course of spontaneous neuromuscular recovery is extremely variable from patient to patient. Due to the highly significant interpatient variability and unpredictability of responses to NMBD administration, quantitative neuromuscular monitoring is required.409,410,491

Neuromuscular Monitoring. Recently published guidelines for perioperative management of neuromuscular block recommend that quantitative neuromuscular monitoring be used to guide perioperative dosing of both NMBDs and

Presence of Cholinesterase Deficiency. The duration of neuromuscular block following the administration of either succinylcholine or mivacurium is primarily determined by their rate of hydrolysis by plasma cholinesterase.

Patients with abnormal plasma cholinesterase phenotypes and activity can demonstrate significant prolongation of clinical effect of these NMBDs. Mivacurium is four to five times more potent in patients phenotypically homozygous for the atypical plasma cholinesterase gene than in patients with normal cholinesterase activity.494 Following standard intubating doses of mivacurium, recovery of neuromuscular function in patients with cholinesterase deficiency may require up to 4 to 8 hours.196 Similar prolonged recovery times have been observed in patients receiving succinylcholine who have atypical plasma cholinesterase genes.495

Human plasma cholinesterase has been used clinically to reverse neuromuscular block in patients with atypical serum cholinesterase. In 1977, Scholler and associates reported data on 15 patients with unexpectedly prolonged apnea lasting several hours after a single dose of succinylcholine.496 Adequate spontaneous ventilation was restored within an average of 10 minutes in all subjects following the administration of human serum cholinesterase. Naguib and associates reported successful reversal of a profound mivacurium- induced neuromuscular block with three doses of a purified human plasma cholinesterase preparation and, in a subsequent study, established a dose–response relationship for plasma cholinesterase as a reversal agent for mivacurium in normal subjects.196,497 The efficacy of exogenously administered plasma cholinesterase in antagonism of a mivacurium neuromuscular block was assessed in 11 patients phenotypically homozygous for atypical plasma cholinesterase.498 A purified concentrate of cholinesterase (2.8–10 mg/kg) was administered 30 or 120 minutes after an intubating dose of mivacurium. Administration of cholinesterase restored plasma cholinesterase to normal levels, resulting in a 9- to 15- fold increased clearance and a shorter elimination half- life of mivacurium. The first response to TOF stimulation was observed in 13.5 minutes, and the time to achieve a TOF ratio of 0.80 ranged from 30 to 60 minutes. These data suggest that prolonged neuromuscular block secondary to low or abnormal plasma cholinesterase

activity can be successfully managed with purified human plasma cholinesterase. Decisions relating to management of prolonged neuromuscular block in patients with atypical plasma cholinesterase should be based on the availability and cost of human plasma cholinesterase versus delaying tracheal extubation (and maintaining an adequate level of anesthesia) until spontaneous neuromuscular recovery has occurred.

Table 24.18 summarizes clinical management strategies that can be used by clinicians to reduce the risk of residual block when nondepolarizing NMBDs are antagonized with anticholinesterases or sugammadex.

Complications Associated with Inhibitors of Acetylcholinesterase ​

Neostigmine- Associated Muscle Weakness. Anticholinesterase agents such as neostigmine possess neuromuscular blocking properties in the absence of previously administered neuromuscular blocking drugs. This has been known since the 1930s when Briscoe conducted experiments on cats and found that neostigmine administered at control (before administration of curare) caused depression of neuromuscular transmission while small doses of curare abolished this neostigmine- induced depression.499 Other investigators confirmed that doses of neostigmine used for antagonism of neuromuscular blocking drugs can produce weakness.500 These and several subsequent studies have demonstrated that neostigmine must be used appropriately and in carefully considered doses. When a nondepolarizing agent occupies sufficient acetylcholine receptors, this low- level block provides a protective effect from the depolarizing actions of excess acetylcholine; patients are at highest risk for neostigmine- induced weakness if they have not previously received a neuromuscular blocking drugs or if they have recovered completely from neuromuscular block. Caldwell et al. examined neostigmine reversal (20 or 40 mcg/kg) of residual neuromuscular block 1 to 4 hours after a single dose of vecuronium.492 TOF ratios increased in 52 patients and decreased in 8 patients; TOF ratio decreases were only observed in patients who received the higher dose of 40 mcg/kg and had pre- reversal TOF ratios 0.90 or greater (Fig. 24.30). In contrast, Murphy and colleagues randomized 90 surgical patients to receive either 40 mcg/kg of neostigmine or saline when TOF ratio of 0.90 to 1.0 was achieved at the end of surgery.493 No decreases in TOF ratios were observed in any patients given neostigmine, and no differences between groups were observed in the incidence of airway obstruction, hypoxemic events, or signs and symptoms of muscle weakness. It is not clear how best to reconcile the studies by Caldwell et al.492 and Murphy et al.493 It has been postulated that in the latter study, in which TOF ratios were measured with acceleromyography, there may have been enough remaining receptor occupancy by NMBD to provide a protective effect from the depolarizing actions of excess acetylcholine. This may be consistent with the general tendency of acceleromyography to overestimate the degree of recovery compared with mechanomyography or electromyography (see Chapter 39).

Pharmacologic reversal, including neostigmine, should not be administered to patients who have an adequate spontaneous recovery to TOF ratio ≥0.9. However, determination of whether adequate recovery has occurred is only

possible with quantitative monitoring. Therefore, in the absence of quantitative monitoring and with a TOF response of 4 twitches without subjective fade, it is advisable to limit the neostigmine dose to 15–30 mcg/kg. The lower dose, 15 mcg/kg, can be considered if it is known that subjective fade at the adductor pollicis has been absent for 30 minutes or more. There is controversy regarding the highest safe dose of neostigmine to be used when the neuromuscular block may be recovered. Although Caldwell et al. found no evidence of weakness after a dose of 20 mcg/kg, the exact level of receptor occupancy was not known. Other investigators administered 30 mcg/kg to volunteers who had recovered from nondepolarizing block to an acceleromyography- measured

TOFR of 1.0 and found increased upper airway collapsibility and impaired genioglossus activation.501 If sugammadex is used for rescue reversal after neostigmine has failed to induce adequate antagonism, there is the potential for complete removal of free rocuronium molecules by sugammadex. If a large dose of neostigmine had previously been administered, the overabundance of acetylcholine may then induce a depolarizing block resulting in impaired neuromuscular transmission and significant muscle weakness. This potential situation should always be considered if there is a need for sugammadex administration following incomplete reversal by neostigmine. It is one more reason for reserving neostigmine for reversal of minimal block (see Table 24.18) and then only using low doses. Neostigmine- induced weakness is thought to be of relatively short duration (<30 minutes), but any weakness in the early postextubation period can have significant adverse consequences. Studies suggest that sugammadex does not appear to produce adverse effects on upper airway tone or normal breathing when given after neuromuscular recovery.502

Nausea and Vomiting. The impact of anticholinesterases on the incidence of postoperative nausea and vomiting remains controversial. Systemic anticholinesterases produce effects outside of the neuromuscular junction that may influence the risk of unwanted side effects following anesthesia and surgery. In addition to the action within the neuromuscular junction, anticholinesterase drugs result in muscarinic effects on the gastrointestinal tract, resulting in stimulation of secretion of gastric fluid and increases in gastric motility. The use of smaller doses of neostigmine in combination with atropine decreases lower esophageal sphincter tone.503 Furthermore, neostigmine may produce nausea and vomiting via a central effect. Intrathecal neostigmine increases the incidence of nausea and vomiting, likely through a direct effect on the brainstem.

Anticholinergic drugs (e.g., atropine, glycopyrrolate) are routinely administered with anticholinesterases to
