attenuate the undesirable muscarinic effects of these reversal agents. It is thought that anticholinergic drugs may have antiemetic properties.504 When given to children receiving sedation (in the absence of anticholinesterases), atropine was associated with significantly less vomiting (5.3%) than either glycopyrrolate (10.7%) or no anticholinergic agent (11.4%).505 Similarly, surgical patients who were randomized to receive atropine had significantly less nausea than those receiving glycopyrrolate.506 Atropine is a tertiary amine that can readily cross the blood–brain barrier and produce central effects, whereas glycopyrrolate is a quaternary amine that does not penetrate the blood–brain barrier. The beneficial effects of atropine on nausea and vomiting are likely secondary to a central nervous system effect.

Several randomized clinical trials have been performed to determine whether anticholinesterase administration results in an increase in the incidence of postoperative nausea and vomiting. Unfortunately, most study populations were small (39–120 patients). Two systematic reviews have been conducted to address this limitation. One review analyzed eight trials with data from 1134 patients and compared antagonism with neostigmine or edrophonium with spontaneous recovery from long- or intermediate- acting NMBDs.507 An analysis of the neostigmine data across all trials and doses revealed no evidence of an increased risk of early and late nausea and vomiting. However, some evidence in adults suggested that antagonism with larger doses of neostigmine (2.5 mg) might increase the incidence of these events. No evidence was found for this effect with edrophonium. A later systematic review evaluated the effect of neostigmine on postoperative nausea and vomiting while considering the different anticholinergics as confounding variables.504 Ten randomized trials (933 patients) that compared neostigmine to inactive control were included. The combination of neostigmine with either glycopyrrolate or atropine did not increase the incidence of nausea or vomiting, nor was there an increased risk when large doses of neostigmine were compared with smaller doses of the drug (Table 24.19). Atropine was associated with a reduction in the risk of vomiting, but glycopyrrolate was not. In conclusion, there is at present insufficient evidence to conclude

that neostigmine or edrophonium is associated with an increased risk of postoperative nausea and vomiting.

Cardiovascular Effects. Pronounced vagal effects are observed following the administration of anticholinesterases. Bradycardia and other bradyarrhythmias, such as junctional rhythms, ventricular escape beats, complete heart block, and asystole, have been reported. The time course of these bradyarrhythmias parallels the onset of action of the anticholinesterases, with the most rapid onset observed with edrophonium, slower for neostigmine, and slowest for pyridostigmine.472 In order to counteract these cardiovascular effects, atropine and glycopyrrolate are administered concurrently with anticholinesterases. Atropine and glycopyrrolate have muscarinic (parasympathetic) blocking effects, but do not block nicotinic receptors. Atropine has a more rapid onset of action (approximately 1 minute) compared with glycopyrrolate (2–3 minutes), although the duration of action of both agents is similar (30–60 minutes). Despite the concurrent administration of anticholinergic drugs, a high incidence of bradyarrhythmia is observed following anticholinesterase reversal (up to 50%–60% of patients in some studies).508 The risk of arrhythmias is influenced by the type of anticholinesterase and anticholinergic used, the dose of anticholinesterase and anticholinergic agent administered, the speed of drug administration (and, therefore, the peak plasma concentration achieved), and background anesthetic used (opioidbased vs. volatile anesthetic and type of NMBD).

Several investigations have examined the heart rate and rhythm responses to various anticholinesterase/anticholinergic combinations. In general, it is preferable to use atropine with edrophonium because the onset of action of both drugs is rapid. Edrophonium–atropine mixtures induced small increases in heart rate, whereas edrophonium–glycopyrrolate mixtures caused decreases in heart rate and occasionally, severe bradycardia.509 Similarly, the onset of cholinergic effects of neostigmine coincides with the onset of the anticholinergic effects of glycopyrrolate; glycopyrrolate is superior to atropine in protecting against neostigmineinduced bradyarrhythmias.510 When atropine is given with

原始表格與符號（PDF 第 53 頁）

edrophonium (0.5–1.0 mg/kg), doses of 5 to 7 mcg/kg are recommended, although larger doses may be used in certain circumstances.509,511 If glycopyrrolate is given with neostigmine, minimal changes in heart rate are observed if a dose equivalent of one-fourth to one-fifth the dose of neostigmine is used (e.g., 0.5 mg glycopyrrolate with 2 to 2.5 mg of neostigmine).510 Because the onset of action is slow with pyridostigmine, tachycardia may be observed when either atropine or glycopyrrolate is co- administered.

More recent investigations have examined the impact of atropine and glycopyrrolate, given with neostigmine, on autonomic control in the postoperative period. During physiologically stressful events, control of heart rate and arterial blood pressure is regulated by the sympathetic and parasympathetic nervous systems. Anticholinergic drugs attenuate the efferent parasympathetic regulation of heart rate and suppress cardiac baroreflex sensitivity and heart rate variability. This suppression of the parasympathetic system may predispose patients to cardiac arrhythmias following surgery. Marked decreases in baroreflex sensitivity and high- frequency heart rate variability have been observed in healthy volunteers given either atropine (20 mcg/kg) or glycopyrrolate (7 mcg/kg).512 Although the times required to return to baseline values were prolonged in both groups, recovery times were significantly longer in the subjects given atropine (177–212 minutes) compared with those given glycopyrrolate (82–111 minutes). Similar effects have been observed in healthy patients undergoing general anesthesia antagonized with neostigmine and anticholinergics.513 Neuromuscular block was antagonized with neostigmine 50 mcg/kg and either atropine 20 mcg/kg or glycopyrrolate 8 mcg/kg. Two hours after receiving neostigmine, patients given atropine had persistent impairment of baroreflex sensitivity and high- frequency heart rate variability, whereas these variables had returned to baseline values in patients receiving glycopyrrolate. These investigations demonstrate that the parasympathetic nervous system control of heart rate is less impaired by glycopyrrolate than by atropine.

Pulmonary Effects – Bronchoconstriction. Bronchospasm can occur after the administration of neostigmine in surgical patients.514,515 Anticholinesterases (e.g., neostigmine) stimulate muscarinic receptors in airway smooth muscle; stimulation of these receptors can provoke bronchoconstriction. Neostigmine and pyridostigmine induce a phosphatidylinositol response (a result of smooth muscle contraction induced by a muscarinic agonist) in airway muscle, which can result in bronchoconstriction.516 This response was inhibited in the presence of atropine, which is a direct bronchodilator. Edrophonium did not induce a phosphatidylinositol response. In patients with cervical spinal cord injuries, neostigmine alone caused bronchoconstriction, whereas neostigmine combined with glycopyrrolate caused bronchodilation.517 The risk of perioperative bronchospasm appears low if neostigmine is administered concurrently with anticholinergics and the risk is potentially further reduced by administering glycopyrrolate 30–60 seconds before neostigmine administration.

SUGAMMADEX ​

Sugammadex is a modified γ- cyclodextrin and the first selective relaxant–binding agent based on an encapsulating

principle for reversal of rocuronium- and vecuroniuminduced neuromuscular block. The complex formation of sugammadex and rocuronium or vecuronium occurs at all levels of neuromuscular block (profound through shallow) and results in a faster pharmacologic reversal when compared with anticholinesterase drugs. Consequently, sugammadex may markedly reduce postoperative residual neuromuscular block in the PACU.518

Structure–Activity Relationships and Mechanism of Action ​

Cyclodextrins consist of several cyclic oligosaccharides and have a three- dimensional structure, which resembles a hollow, truncated cone or doughnut.519,520 The structure has a hydrophobic cavity and hydrophilic exterior because of the presence of polar hydroxyl groups. Hydrophobic interactions trap the lipophilic molecules in the cyclodextrin cavity, thereby resulting in the formation of a water- soluble guest–host complex. The stability of the rocuronium– sugammadex complex is a result of the combination of intermolecular forces (van der Waals forces), including thermodynamic (hydrogen bonds) and hydrophobic interactions (Fig. 24.31).520–522 Sugammadex forms a rigid complex in a 1:1 ratio with steroidal NMBDs (rocuronium and vecuronium) (Fig. 24.32).520 There is some binding affinity with pancuronium, but this interaction is too low to have a significant clinical effect. The molecular mass of the sugammadex–rocuronium complex is 2532 g/mol (sugammadex 2002 g/mol and rocuronium 530 g/mol), and that of the sugammadex–vecuronium complex is 2640 g/mol (vecuronium 638 g/mol).520 The rocuronium–sugammadex complex exists in an equilibrium with an association/ dissociation rate of 1 molar concentration of sugammadex and rocuronium of 25,000,000:1, which means that sugammadex forms a very tight complex that encapsulates rocuronium at 25 million times the rate of dissociation of one molecule complex. The affinity of sugammadex toward vecuronium is 2.5 times lower, but still sufficiently high to form a tight complex.520 Rapid binding of rocuronium and sugammadex results in removal of free rocuronium molecules from the plasma. This creates a concentration gradient favoring movement of the remaining rocuronium molecules from the effect site at the neuromuscular junction back into the plasma, where the drug is encapsulated by the remaining free sugammadex molecules. Neuromuscular block is quickly reversed as rocuronium is removed from the binding sites at the neuromuscular junction. Sugammadex administration results in an increase in the total plasma concentration of rocuronium (free and bound to sugammadex).523 Because sugammadex acts as a selective binding agent and has no direct or indirect action on the molecular components of cholinergic transmission (cholinesterase, nicotinic receptors, or muscarinic receptors), the need for co- administration of anticholinergic drugs is eliminated.524

Pharmacokinetics ​

The pharmacokinetic profile of sugammadex and rocuronium has been investigated in healthy volunteers and surgical patients.525 Sugammadex, in a dose range of 0.1 to 8.0 mg/kg in healthy adult volunteers (without neuromuscular block), exhibited a dose- linear pharmacokinetic profile, a volume distribution of 18 L, an elimination

Fig. 24.31 Structure of the synthetic γ- cyclodextrin sugammadex (Org 25969). (From Bom A, Bradley M, Cameron K, et al. A novel concept of reversing neuromuscular block. Chemical encapsulating of rocuronium bromide by a cyclodextrin- based synthetic host. Angew Chem. 2002;41:266–270.)

Fig. 24.32 The sugammadex–rocuronium complex. (From Bom A, Bradley M, Cameron K, et al. A novel concept of reversing neuromuscular block. Chemical encapsulating of rocuronium bromide by a cyclodextrinbased synthetic host. Angew Chem. 2002;41:266–270.)

half- life of 100 minutes, and a plasma clearance rate of 120 mL/min, with up to 80% of the dose being excreted in urine over 24 hours.525 After encapsulation by sugammadex, rocuronium is less free to distribute to compartments other than those in which sugammadex resides. During an infusion of rocuronium to maintain a stable depth of neuromuscular block, administration of sugammadex increased the measured plasma concentration of rocuronium; rocuronium redistributed from the effect compartment (including the neuromuscular junction) to the central compartment (mostly as the sugammadex complex) as it was encapsulated by sugammadex.523 The volume

of distribution of rocuronium decreases with increasing doses of sugammadex until the volume of distribution of rocuronium approaches the volume of distribution of sugammadex at higher doses.523 Encapsulation changes the pharmacokinetics of rocuronium. In the absence of sugammadex, rocuronium is eliminated mainly by biliary excretion (>75%) and to a lesser degree by renal excretion (10%–25%).526 The main difference in the pharmacokinetic profile of sugammadex and rocuronium is that the clearance of sugammadex is approximately three times slower than that of rocuronium.525 The rate and amount of urinary excretion of rocuronium when administered alone is slow and small, but when sugammadex (a dose of 2.0 mg/kg or more) is administered, the plasma clearance of rocuronium is decreased by a factor of more than two.525 This decreased clearance occurs because the biliary route of excretion becomes unavailable for the rocuronium–sugammadex complex as the large size of this complex prohibits additional renal excretion. The clearance of rocuronium after binding by sugammadex decreases to a value approaching the GFR (120 mL/min).526 However, the renal excretion of rocuronium is increased by more than 100% after administration of 4.0 to 8.0 mg/kg of sugammadex.526 Following the administration of sugammadex, encapsulation of rocuronium in the plasma results in a rapid decrease in free rocuronium in this compartment, although the total plasma concentration of rocuronium (both free and bound by sugammadex) increases. This results in a concentration gradient between the relatively high level of free rocuronium in the effect compartment (the neuromuscular junction) and the low level in the plasma compartment.523 As a result, free rocuronium molecules rapidly diffuse towards the plasma compartment and are encapsulated by sugammadex. Thus, the increase in plasma levels of rocuronium after sugammadex administration illustrates the mechanism responsible for the rapid reversal of neuromuscular block by sugammadex.

Because renal excretion is the primary route for the elimination of sugammadex and the rocuronium–sugammadex complex, studies on elimination by dialysis have considerable relevance in clinical practice. In a small subset of patients with severe renal impairment, the clearance of sugammadex and rocuronium in blood was 78 and 89 mL/ min, respectively. Therefore, hemodialysis using a highflux dialysis method is effective in removing sugammadex and the sugammadex–rocuronium complex in patients with severe renal impairment.527

Pharmacodynamics ​

Clinical Use of Sugammadex in Healthy Patients. The first human exposure of sugammadex in male volunteers showed a large dose- dependent, more rapid recovery time from a rocuronium- induced neuromuscular block with sugammadex (0.1–8.0 mg/kg) as compared with placebo.525 Further research showed that sugammadex 4 mg/kg was able to antagonize a rocuronium- induced (after a bolus dose of 0.6 mg/kg) neuromuscular block to a TOF ratio of 0.90 in less than 4 minutes.525 Sugammadex reduced the median recovery time in a dose- dependent manner from 21 minutes in the placebo group to 1.1 minutes in the group receiving 4.0 mg/kg of sugammadex.528 In another study, administration of sugammadex resulted in a more rapid and effective recovery from a rocuronium

(0.6 mg/kg) or vecuronium (0.1 mg/kg) neuromuscular block (Figs. 24.33 and 24.34).529 Reversal of neuromuscular block with larger doses of rocuronium (1.0–1.2 mg/kg) by different doses of sugammadex (2.0–16.0 mg/kg) at different time points (3–15 minutes after rocuronium) showed

a dose- dependent, rapid, and effective reversal compared with placebo.60,530–532

Whereas anticholinesterase drugs, such as neostigmine, are unable to antagonize deeper levels of neuromuscular block (e.g., PTC of 1–2), sugammadex is effective in reversing deep and complete neuromuscular block.60,533 Therefore reversal of moderate and deep rocuronium and vecuronium neuromuscular blocks can be achieved reliably by administration of sugammadex, provided a dose of 2.0 and 4.0 mg/kg, respectively, is used (Table 24.20).60,529–533

In contrast to the anticholinesterase drugs (e.g., neostigmine), complete neuromuscular block (no response to TOF and PTC stimulation) can be antagonized by sugammadex shortly after the administration of rocuronium. In a multicenter investigation, patients were randomized to receive 1.2 mg/kg of rocuronium followed 3 minutes later by 16 mg/kg of sugammadex, or received a dose of 1.0 mg/ kg of succinylcholine.534 The mean time to 90% recovery of the first twitch (T1) from the start of sugammadex administration was 2.9 minutes and to a recovery of the TOF ratio to 0.90 was 2.2 minutes.534 In contrast, the spontaneous recovery time from a succinylcholine neuromuscular block to 90% recovery of T1 was 10.9 minutes. Thus, reversal of large doses of rocuronium (used for rapid- sequence induction and intubation) with 16 mg/kg sugammadex was significantly faster than spontaneous recovery from succinylcholine (Fig. 24.35).534 These findings were confirmed in a randomized trial that assessed how rapidly spontaneous ventilation could be reestablished after rapid-sequence induction of anesthesia and intubation of the trachea, using either the combination of rocuronium (1.0 mg/kg) plus sugammadex (16 mg/kg) or succinylcholine (1.0 mg/ kg).535 The median time from tracheal intubation to spontaneous ventilation was 406 seconds with succinylcholine and 216 seconds with rocuronium–sugammadex (Table 24.21).535 These data demonstrated that sugammadex reversal of a large- dose rocuronium neuromuscular block was not only significantly faster than spontaneous recovery from succinylcholine, but also that spontaneous ventilation could be restored more rapidly (i.e., this dose can be used to replace succinylcholine for endotracheal intubation). In clinical practice and during an unexpected difficult airway (e.g., “cannot intubate, cannot ventilate” scenario), a rocuronium neuromuscular block may be reversed by sugammadex immediately to rapidly restore spontaneous ventilation, depending on body habitus, duration of oxygen

administration, and provided that the induction doses of propofol and opioids were not excessive.536

When sugammadex was compared with neostigmine or edrophonium, the time course of neuromuscular recovery was markedly different.537–539 In a clinical study, patients received 0.6 mg/kg of rocuronium, after which neuromuscular block was sustained with supplemental boluses of rocuronium given at the reappearance of the second twitch (second response to TOF stimulation, or T2).537 Fifteen minutes after the last dose of rocuronium, either 70 mcg/ kg of neostigmine, 1 mg/kg of edrophonium, or 4.0 mg/ kg sugammadex was administered. The average time to achieve a TOF ratio of 0.90 was 10 times longer after the administration of neostigmine than it was after sugammadex (1044 seconds vs. 107 seconds) and 3 times longer after the administration of edrophonium (331 seconds). Similar differences were found when comparing reversal of rocuronium at the reappearance of the second twitch in the TOF response using 2 mg/kg sugammadex versus neostigmine 50 mcg/kg.538 This was also confirmed in an investigation that assessed the efficacy of sugammadex versus neostigmine for reversal of profound rocuronium- induced

neuromuscular block.539 More than 97% of patients who received sugammadex (4.0 mg/kg) at a PTC of 1 to 2 recovered to a TOF ratio of 0.90 within 5 minutes. In contrast, 73% of the patients who received neostigmine (70 mcg/kg) recovered between 30 and 60 minutes after administration, with 23% of patients requiring more than 60 minutes to recover to a TOF ratio of 0.90 (Fig. 24.36).

A randomized trial compared the efficacy of sugammadex reversal of a rocuronium (0.6 mg/kg) neuromuscular block with that of neostigmine reversal of a cisatracurium (0.15 mg/kg) neuromuscular block.540 Time from the start of administration of reversal agent sugammadex 2.0 mg/ kg or neostigmine 50 mcg/kg to recovery of the TOF ratio to 0.90 was 4.7 times faster with sugammadex than with neostigmine: 1.9 versus 9.0 minutes, respectively.

While pipecuronium has been withdrawn from the market in several countries after the introduction of intermediate- acting neuromuscular blocking drugs, the approval of sugammadex has in turn reintroduced pipecuronium into clinical anesthesia practice. In fact, sugammadex has a greater binding affinity for pipecuronium than for other steroid- type neuromuscular blocking drugs.541 In

a placebo- controlled, double- blind, dose- finding trial, doses of 1, 2, 3, or 4 mg/kg of sugammadex reversed moderate (TOF count of 2) pipecuronium block within 5 minutes in all patients. Moreover, 79% of the patients recovered to a normalized TOF ratio 0.9 within 2 minutes.542 In another trial, 2 mg/kg sugammadex proved to be noninferior to 4 mg/kg for reversal of deep (PTC of 1) pipecuronium block. The time to reach normalized TOF ratio 0.9 was 1.73 ± 1.03 min in the 2 mg/kg sugammadex patient group, and 1.42 ±

0.63 min in the 4 mg/kg sugammadex group, respectively. The mean difference in reversal times between the 2 groups was 0.31 min (95% CI, −0.18 to 0.8).541 Reappearance of neuromuscular block (“recurarization”) postoperatively was not observed in either study.

Unlike with neostigmine or edrophonium, the choice of anesthetic (e.g., propofol vs. sevoflurane) does not influence the ability of sugammadex to reverse rocuronium- induced neuromuscular block.543,544 Provided that sugammadex is used in recommended dosages according to the level of neuromuscular block, a small risk exists of incomplete neuromuscular recovery or reoccurrence of neuromuscular block following surgery.

Clinical Use of Sugammadex in Pediatric Patients. The use of sugammadex in pediatric patients was examined in a study enrolling 8 infants (28 days to 23 months), 24 children (2–11 years), and 31 adolescents (12–17 years).545 Patients were anesthetized with propofol and opioids and received rocuronium 0.6 mg/kg. At the reappearance of T2 (second twitch of TOF), patients received sugammadex 0.5, 1.0, 2.0, or 4.0 mg/kg or placebo. Recovery time to a TOF ratio of 0.90 decreased in a dose- dependent manner in all age groups. Residual neuromuscular block or recurarization was not observed, and no side effects were reported.

A large case–control study from a Japanese national database found no association between sugammadex and perioperative anaphylaxis in pediatric patients who underwent general anesthesia.546 In a recent study that included 276 patients aged 2 to 16 years, recovery to a TOF ratio >0.9 from rocuronium- or vecuronium- induced neuromuscular block was significantly faster with 2 mg/ kg sugammadex than with neostigmine. Moreover, time

原始表格與符號（PDF 第 58 頁）

to reversal of deep neuromuscular block with 4 mg/kg sugammadex was consistent with that of moderate neuromuscular block. No differences in clinically relevant bradycardia, hypersensitivity, or anaphylaxis were reported with sugammadex when compared with neostigmine.547 A recent meta- analysis has demonstrated that sugammadex was associated with a lower incidence of postoperative nausea and vomiting, bradycardia, and dry mouth, compared to neostigmine.548 While sugammadex can be used safely in children and adolescents (2–17 years old), information on the use of sugammadex in pediatric patients less than 2 years old is still limited.549

Clinical Use of Sugammadex in Older Adult Patients. ​

Reversal of neuromuscular block by sugammadex has been assessed in older patients. One hundred and fifty patients were divided into three groups; an adult group (18–64 years old), an older adult group (65–75 years old), and an oldest adult group (75 years or older).550 Patients received an intubating dose of rocuronium 0.6 mg/kg with maintenance doses of 0.15 mg/kg as required. Sugammadex 2.0 mg/kg was administered after the last dose of rocuronium at the reappearance of T2. Recovery of neuromuscular block facilitated by sugammadex was slightly (0.7 minute) faster in patients younger than 65 years of age. In general, a prolonged circulation time secondary to a reduced cardiac output in older patients was anticipated to result in a longer recovery time from neuromuscular block after administration of sugammadex.551,552 However, based on these results, no dose adjustments are needed in older patients.550 Yet, if it is necessary to achieve a fast recovery in a short period of time, a higher dose of sugammadex might be considered.553

Clinical Use of Sugammadex in Special Patient Populations ​

cardiac disease. Studies evaluating the safety and efficacy of sugammadex in patients with underlying cardiovascular disease have not demonstrated an effect of sugammadex on electrocardiogram (no indication of a possible prolongation of the QTc interval).554,555 A study designed to evaluate the effects of sugammadex on QTc prolongation in healthy subjects (in doses up to 32 mg/kg, alone or in combination with rocuronium or vecuronium) revealed that the administration of sugammadex was not associated with QTc prolongation.555 In a recent large retrospective cohort analysis that investigated sugammadex vs. neostigmine antagonism, the most common side effect was bradycardia: 2.4% vs. 2.2%, respectively.556 Compared with neostigmine–glycopyrrolate, the incidence of sinus bradycardia was significantly lower after 2 mg/ kg sugammadex, and the incidence of sinus tachycardia was significantly lower with 2 and 4 mg/kg sugammadex in ASA physical status 3 or 4 patients.557 Based on current data, sugammadex antagonism is not associated with cardiovascular side effects in healthy patients or in those with cardiovascular comorbidities (see also earlier section, Complications Associated With Inhibitors of Acetylcholinesterase).

pulmonary disease. Patients with a history of pulmonary disease have an increased risk of postoperative pulmonary complications such as pneumonia, respiratory

failure, and exacerbation of the underlying pulmonary disease.558 Sugammadex has been studied in patients with pulmonary disease.558 Seventy- seven surgical patients with a diagnosis or known history of pulmonary disorders received sugammadex in doses up to 4 mg/kg to reverse a rocuronium neuromuscular block. As in other adult patient groups, antagonism of a rocuronium- induced neuromuscular block was rapid, and there were no signs of residual neuromuscular block or re- paralysis.559 Of the 77 patients treated with sugammadex, 2 patients developed bronchospasm, at 1 minute and 55 minutes after the administration of sugammadex. Both patients were asthmatic, and there was no evidence that these symptoms were related to sugammadex. In other subsets of high- risk pulmonary patients (cystic fibrosis and end- stage lung disease), the successful use of sugammadex has been reported.560 Antagonism of neuromuscular block with sugammadex has potential advantages compared with anticholinesterase drugs (e.g., neostigmine) in patients with pulmonary disease because sugammadex lacks interactions with the muscarinic cholinergic system, and there is no need for coadministration of anticholinergic drugs (also see section titled Complications Associated With Inhibitors of Acetylcholinesterase, above). In patients at high risk of pulmonary complications, the use of sugammadex is associated with a reduced risk of development of postoperative pneumonia or respiratory failure.561

renal failure. The use of sugammadex to antagonize rocuronium neuromuscular block was investigated in 15 patients with severe renal impairment (creatinine clearance <30 mL/min), compared with 15 patients with normal renal function (creatinine clearance >80 mL/ min).562 Sugammadex 2 mg/kg was administered at the reappearance of T2. There were no differences between groups in the recovery profile or the incidence of residual block after sugammadex (Table 24.22). In another study, the reversal of a rocuronium- induced deep neuromuscular block with sugammadex 4.0 mg/kg in patients with severe renal impairment (creatinine clearance <30 mL/ min) required 3.1 minutes, compared with the control normal renal function patients who required 1.9 minutes (median difference between groups was 1.3 minutes).563 Sugammadex and sugammadex–rocuronium complex exposure is increased in patients with moderate and severe renal failure due to the decreased renal clearance.563,564 The use of sugammadex in renal transplantation was described in two cases in which sugammadex was used for antagonism of a rocuronium- induced neuromuscular block in pediatric patients; antagonism was fast and complete without signs of residual neuromuscular block or reparalysis.565 In another study, the long- term efficacy and safety of rocuronium and sugammadex in patients undergoing renal transplantation were described in 57 patients diagnosed with severe renal failure.566 Rocuronium and sugammadex appeared to be efficacious and safe in patients undergoing renal transplantation. Because complete elimination of the sugammadex–rocuronium complex remains poorly understood in renal impairment, sugammadex is at present not recommended for use in patients with severe renal failure. However, it can be used in patients with mild or moderate renal dysfunction as its safety profile is similar to that of healthy patients.562 A cohort study that

included 219 patients has demonstrated that patients with a GFR <15 mL/min tolerated sugammadex antagonism well.567 Theoretically, because of the molecular mass of the rocuronium/vecuronium–sugammadex complex, it is possible to decrease the plasma levels of this complex by dialysis. Hemodialysis using a high- flux dialysis method has been shown to be effective in removing sugammadex and the sugammadex–rocuronium complex in patients with severe renal impairment.527

hepatobiliary disease. Sugammadex has been sparsely studied in animal models or in patients with hepatic impairment. However, it is known that the biliary route of excretion becomes unavailable for either sugammadex or the rocuronium/vecuronium–sugammadex complex because the large size of this complex prohibits such excretion.558 A population pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK- PD) model was used to simulate the scenario of an immediate antagonism, and reversal of a profound rocuronium- induced neuromuscular block in patients with hepatic impairment.558 Under such study conditions, hepatic impairment had little effect on the reversal time when sugammadex (16 mg/kg) was administered 3 minutes after rocuronium (1.2 mg/kg). However, in other scenarios (sugammadex 2 mg/kg at reappearance of T2 and 4 mg/kg after 15 minutes), recovery from a rocuroniuminduced (1.2 mg/kg) neuromuscular block was predicted to be longer than that seen in healthy patients.558 Sugammadex antagonized rocuronium- induced neuromuscular block efficiently (TOF count of 2 measured at the adductor pollicis muscle) in patients with Child class “A” liver cirrhosis, and was associated with a 80% reduction in time to adequate recovery (3.1 minutes) when compared to neostigmine (14.5 minutes).568

obesity. Patients with obesity, particularly morbid obesity (body mass index [BMI] >40 kg/m2 ), are at risk for cardiovascular and respiratory complications perioperatively.569 These patients are susceptible to critical respiratory events in the postoperative period, including hypoventilation, hypoxia, airway obstruction, and acute respiratory failure.425,452 The presence of postoperative residual neuromuscular block may further increase the risk for postoperative complications in these patients by producing impairment of the integrity of the upper airway leading to upper airway collapse.138,423 Therefore, complete antagonism of neuromuscular block must be achieved before tracheal extubation is attempted. In this setting, sugammadex may have a more favorable recovery

profile than traditional anticholinesterase drugs because it provides a more reliable recovery of neuromuscular function and a lower risk of residual neuromuscular block.569 A key issue is determining the appropriate dose of sugammadex to administer to a morbidly obese patient that is sufficient to capture all remaining free NMBD molecules. While the dosing of NMBDs in obese patients should be based on lean/ideal body weight (because these drugs are hydrophilic and their volume of distribution is minimally affected by obesity), the dosing of sugammadex in obese patients is currently under debate. To ensure complete neuromuscular recovery, the dose of sugammadex must be sufficient to affect the gradient between the peripheral and central compartments and effectively encapsulate all free rocuronium molecules. An inadequate dose of sugammadex may be incapable of sustaining this redistribution of rocuronium and may lead to reoccurrence of neuromuscular block.

The current product monograph recommends calculating the sugammadex dose based on the patient’s actual body weight. However, because the low volume of distribution at steady state (estimated as 0.16 L/kg) restricts distribution to the intravascular space, it might be relevant to determine the dose of sugammadex based on the lean/ ideal body weight and not on the actual body weight.562 However, in a recently published pooled analysis (27 trials) on the use of sugammadex in patients with obesity (BMI >30 kg/m2 ), the authors concluded that the recommended dose based on actual body weight provided rapid recovery from neuromuscular block in both obese and nonobese patients and that no dose adjustments should be made in the obese patient.570 Several studies have investigated the dose of sugammadex based on lean or variations on the lean/ideal body weight.571–574 In one investigation, a sugammadex dose of 4 mg/kg based on a lean/ideal body weight calculation was administered to morbidly obese patients during a profound rocuronium neuromuscular block.573 Approximately 40% of these patients had inadequate reversal. In these patients, an additional dose of sugammadex 2 mg/kg based on lean/ideal body weight was required to achieve a TOF ratio 0.90. The authors concluded that a sugammadex dose based on lean/ideal body weight was insufficient for antagonizing both profound and moderate block in a considerable number of morbidly obese patients.573

In another study in morbidly obese patients, investigators examined reversal of a moderate level of a rocuronium

neuromuscular block (at T1–T2) using a dose of sugammadex of 2.0 mg/kg.572 Four different weight corrections were used: lean/ideal body weight, lean/ideal body weight + 20%, lean/ideal body weight + 40%, and actual body weight. This study demonstrated that a moderate rocuronium neuromuscular block could be effectively antagonized with sugammadex 2.0 mg/kg using the calculation lean/ideal body weight + 40%.572 These findings were confirmed in a recent study.575 However, larger interindividual variability of recovery times occur when dosing is based on lean/ideal body weight compared with dosing based on actual body weight.572 Additionally, reoccurrence of neuromuscular block after suboptimal dosing of sugammadex has been reported in a morbidly obese patient.574 Hence, until more data are available, the dose of sugammadex should be based on the actual body weight.

pregnancy and cesarean section. Induction of general anesthesia in patients undergoing cesarean section typically involves a rapid-sequence induction of anesthesia with either thiopental or propofol and a rapid- onset NMBD. For decades, succinylcholine has been the prototypic NMBD used in these procedures to produce rapid optimal endotracheal intubation conditions.576 Rocuronium is an acceptable alternative to succinylcholine in rapid-sequence induction of anesthesia procedures; rocuronium in doses larger than 1.0 mg/kg not only provides onset of action within 60 seconds but also provides identical intubation conditions compared with succinylcholine.577 However, the duration of action of rocuronium in doses of 1.0 mg/kg or greater will result in a profound neuromuscular block of long duration (often more than 2 hours). Furthermore, the risk of failed intubation in the obstetric population is at least eight times higher compared with nonpregnant females.578 In case of a failed endotracheal intubation scenario or a “cannot intubate, cannot ventilate” situation, rocuronium, even in doses up to 1.2 mg/kg, can be immediately antagonized with sugammadex 16 mg/kg.534

Preclinical animal data demonstrated that uteroplacental transfer of sugammadex is very small (<2% to 6%). Sugammadex does not have negative effects on pregnancy or on embryonic, fetal, or postnatal development.576,579,580 Although no data are available about the excretion of sugammadex in human breast milk, excretion in breast milk is likely minimal, with insignificant clinical impact because oral absorption of cyclodextrins in general is small. Therefore, sugammadex can be used in breastfeeding females. In two case series that examined obstetric patients who received rocuronium and sugammadex (a total of 25

patients), no side effects were observed.579,580 The efficacy and safety of sugammadex in obstetric anesthesia have not been determined; however, serious adverse events for the mother or the neonate have not been reported after sugammadex.

neuromuscular disorders. Neuromuscular disorders are frequently associated with an increased incidence of perioperative respiratory complications due to muscle weakness.581,582 In these patient groups, administration of succinylcholine is often contraindicated because it is associated with potentially life- threatening side effects. The use of nondepolarizing NMBDs can on occasion be associated with prolonged spontaneous neuromuscular recovery, even after a single dose. Consequently, these patients have an increased risk for postoperative muscle weakness of multifactorial origin, including residual neuromuscular block.581,582 Prompt, complete, and reliable recovery of neuromuscular function is essential to optimize patient safety and reduce the risk of pulmonary complications. However, reversal with anticholinesterase (e.g., neostigmine), especially in neuromuscular disorders, can be associated with postoperative complications.582

Multiple case reports and case series describe the use of sugammadex in patients with various neuromuscular disorders such as myasthenia gravis, myotonic dystrophy, and spinal muscular atrophy (Fig. 24.37).582–587 In general, dosing regimens for sugammadex were consistent with recommended doses adjusted to actual body weight and based on the level of neuromuscular block at the time of administration. Sugammadex administration resulted in prompt reversal of neuromuscular block with a similar recovery profile as observed in normal patients. Although no studies have been performed in patients with neuromuscular disorders, the reported cases indicate that sugammadex should be considered as an alternative reversal drug (e.g., instead of neostigmine) in this patient population. These observations need to be confirmed in a larger series of patient studies.

Side Effects and Drug Interactions ​

Hypersensitivity. Sugammadex is contraindicated in patients with known hypersensitivity to the drug. The potential for hypersensitivity is reported in several case reports. Hypersensitivity is a concern; however, this is difficult to study because hypersensitivity reactions occur rarely. In a retrospective analysis, the incidence of potential sugammadex- induced anaphylaxis at a single center over a period of 3 years was investigated. The overall incidence of intraoperative hypersensitivity was 0.22%

The time to spontaneous recovery from the first profound rocuronium- induced neuromuscular block to a train- of- four (TOF) ratio was 36.5 minutes. The time from the start of the administration of sugammadex 4.0 mg/kg after the second dose of rocuronium to the recovery of the TOF ratio to 0.90 was 2.7 minutes. Blue tracings represent T1 recovery while red dots represent TOF ratio recovery. Black dashed line indicates hand skin temperature (°C). (From de Boer HD, van Egmond J, Driessen JJ, et al. (95% CI, 0.17%–0.29%), and the incidence of anaphylaxis was 0.059% (95% CI, 0.032%–0.10%). In total, 15,479 patients were exposed to sugammadex in the study period and the incidence of anaphylaxis associated with sugammadex administration was 0.039% ( n = 6; 95% CI, 0.014%– 0.084%).588 In a randomized study of healthy volunteers, sugammadex hypersensitivity and its potential mechanisms were investigated. Hypersensitivity was adjudicated for 1/148 (0.7%), 7/150 (4.7%), and 0/150 (0.0%) subjects after sugammadex 4 mg/kg, 16 mg/kg, and placebo, respectively.589 Furthermore, laboratory investigations do not suggest prevalent allergen- specific IgE/IgG- mediated immunologic hypersensitivity.589 In a recent review of the literature on sugammadex and anaphylaxis, 33 published cases were identified and evaluated. The average onset time was 3.08 minutes (median 3 minutes, range 1–8 min). The most common signs and symptoms were hypotension, tachycardia, erythema, and desaturation.590

However, more prospective studies are needed to establish the true incidence of sugammadex anaphylaxis. Elevation of serum tryptase (positive predictive value of 93%, negative predictive value of 54%), skin testing (gold standard), and histamine levels in serum and urine are useful to confirm the diagnosis. Recently, a promising test, the basophil activation test, has been established to detect the causative compound of anaphylaxis with high specificity and sensitivity. However, more studies are needed to confirm this method to identify sugammadex- induced anaphylaxis.591

Other Side Effects. Other reported side effects associated with sugammadex administration include coughing, movement, parosmia (abnormal sense of smell), and elevated levels of N - acetyl- glucosaminidase in the urine.558 Coughing and movement after the administration of sugammadex may be due to the unmasking of inadequate anesthesia rather than a direct side effect of sugammadex. Initial studies also reported a prolongation of activated partial thromboplastin and prothrombin time in healthy volunteers after sugammadex administration. However, in a more in- depth patient study that investigated the effect of sugammadex on postsurgical bleeding and coagulation variables, sugammadex produced a limited, transient (<1 hour) increase in activated partial thromboplastin and prothrombin time but was not associated with increased risk of bleeding versus usual care.592

Cyclodextrins are a class of agents well known for their ability to form inclusion complexes with other compounds. Sugammadex forms a very tight complex with rocuronium or vecuronium in a 1:1 molecular ratio; however, because of its mechanism of action, it is possible that other relevant drug interactions may occur.593 Theoretically, two important drug interactions can take place. First, sugammadex is capable of encapsulating endogenous or pharmaceutical molecules other than steroidal NMBDs, resulting in reduced efficacy of the bound molecules. However, the ability to form complexes with steroidal or nonsteroidal molecules such as cortisone, atropine, and verapamil is clinically insignificant because their affinity for sugammadex is 120 to 700 times lower than that of rocuronium.593 In preclinical studies, an interaction of sugammadex with other steroidal compounds could be excluded up to a dose of sugammadex of 500 mg/kg/day.594 If the affinity of sugammadex for

another molecule is very high (higher than the affinity for rocuronium or vecuronium), this molecule may displace the aminosteroid NMBAs from the sugammadex complex, resulting in reoccurrence of neuromuscular block. Both drug interactions may have potential clinical safety implications.593 A modeling approach has been developed to evaluate 300 compounds (including the most commonly used drugs in the perioperative period) for possible displacement interactions with sugammadex.593 In this screening, three compounds were identified as having possible displacement interactions: toremifene, fusidic acid, and flucloxacillin.593 However, no clinically relevant reoccurrence of neuromuscular block was identified when sugammadex was used in combination with these drugs.593 A clinical study reported that flucloxacillin did not cause reoccurrence of neuromuscular block after reversal with sugammadex; no clinically important displacement interaction was observed.595

Use of Contraceptive Drugs by Female Patients. Sugammadex may interact with hormonal contraceptive drugs. Possible capturing interactions, whereby unwanted encapsulation of a third drug by sugammadex reduces its clinical efficacy, have been investigated. In pharmacokinetic–pharmacodynamic simulations, it was predicted that 34% of (free) etonogestrel might be captured by 4 mg/kg sugammadex under very conservative modeling assumption conditions.594 The interaction with this bolus dose of sugammadex resulted in a decrease in etonogestrel exposure, which was similar to the decrease seen after one missed daily dose of an oral contraceptive. Despite a decade of clinical use, little is known about potential adverse effects of sugammadex in women who are lactating or using hormonal contraception. Therefore, it is recommended that women who are using hormonal contraceptives must use an additional nonhormonal or backup contraceptive method for 7 days after sugammadex exposure.596

Special Considerations ​

Reintubation of the Trachea After Reversal of Neuromuscular Block With Sugammadex. Patients receiving sugammadex before extubation of the trachea who need reintubation require special consideration because the remaining circulating sugammadex molecules may potentially interfere with re- administration of rocuronium or vecuronium. In this setting, two alternate strategies can reestablish a neuromuscular block. Within 24 hours of sugammadex administration, it is currently recommended that a benzylisoquinolinium (nonsteroidal) NMBD such as cisatracurium be used instead of rocuronium or vecuronium. This conservative approach is based on the maximum clearance time for sugammadex. However, preclinical and clinical studies have shown that it is possible to safely reestablish neuromuscular block with rocuronium earlier than 24 hours.597 A modeling- based study in healthy volunteers revealed that high- dose rocuronium given 5 to 60 minutes after sugammadex reversal produced a complete neuromuscular block (T1 = 0%).598 Rocuronium (1.2 mg/kg) administrated 5 minutes after sugammadex reversal produced a rapid onset of neuromuscular block (T1 = 0%), with a mean onset time of approximately 3 minutes. Thirty minutes after administration of sugammadex, an

onset time of 1.5 minutes can be achieved with rocuronium (1.2 mg/kg). Hence an inverse relationship exists between the onset time and the time interval between sugammadex and the repeat dose of rocuronium, and a direct relationship exists between the duration of neuromuscular block and the time interval between sugammadex and the repeat dose of rocuronium.

Based on dose calculations using a model in which the equilibrium is described in the common volume of distribution of rocuronium and sugammadex, even a second reversal is possible using sugammadex with large doses between 8 and 20 mg/kg.597

Incomplete Reversal of Neuromuscular Block. Although sugammadex encapsulates rocuronium and vecuronium to form a rigid complex, case reports have described incomplete antagonism of neuromuscular block.530,599 In a dose- finding study, a case was described in which a temporary decrease in the TOF response was observed in a healthy patient after reversal with 0.5 mg/ kg sugammadex.599 The TOF ratio initially reached 0.70 before decreasing to 0.30 and then gradually increased to 0.90 (Fig. 24.38). The authors hypothesized that the decrease in TOF ratio occurred because of redistribution of unbound (free) rocuronium from the peripheral compartments, with insufficient plasma sugammadex available for additional encapsulation of rocuronium. Similarly, incomplete reversal was reported in two healthy patients given suboptimal doses of sugammadex (0.5 mg/kg) during profound rocuronium- induced neuromuscular block.530 In a study by Duvaldestin and associates, reversal of deep neuromuscular block with a low dose of sugammadex (0.5 and 1.0 mg/kg) found that five patients experienced recurrence of neuromuscular block.544 In another study, both speed of reversal and effectiveness of sugammadex were found to be dose- dependent; a single dose of 1.0 mg/kg of sugammadex ( n = 15) administered at a deep neuromuscular block

(PTC = 1) required significantly longer time for antagonism than a dose of 4.0 mg/kg ( n = 60).600 Interestingly, seven patients also exhibited residual ( n = 3) and recurrence ( n = 4) of neuromuscular block in the group of patients who received 1.0 mg/kg sugammadex. No patients who received the recommended dose of 4.0 mg/kg of sugammadex had residual neuromuscular block or re- paralysis. A similar pattern was observed with vecuronium- induced neuromuscular block. A longer reversal time and a higher incidence of residual and/or recurrence of neuromuscular block were noted in patients who received smaller (0.5 and 1.0 mg/kg) doses of sugammadex.601 Therefore, it is recommended that the dose be adjusted according to the depth of neuromuscular block, because underdosing of sugammadex is associated with an increased risk of residual neuromuscular block or recurrence of neuromuscular block.

Antagonism of Deep Block. Historically, an important strategy in anesthesia has been to ensure that neuromuscular block is sufficiently recovered to achieve adequate antagonism using neostigmine. However, when using neostigmine to antagonize a deep neuromuscular block at the end of surgery likely results in residual block. With the availability of sugammadex, complete or deep levels of block (see Table 24.6) have been recommended for the entire duration of laparoscopy. A complete neuromuscular block increases surgical space and requires lower pressures for the pneumoperitoneum.602 It has been postulated that reducing insufflation pressures may improve patient outcomes.602 Other investigators603 have postulated that a deep or complete neuromuscular block may be associated with improved surgical conditions, leading to less postoperative pain, nausea, and vomiting. A recent review and meta- analysis has reported that deep neuromuscular block during laparoscopic surgical procedures allows lower insufflation pressure, thereby improving surgical conditions and reducing postoperative pain.604 Sugammadex, at doses of 2 to 8 mg/kg, was given to antagonize the block if the TOF ratio was less than 0.90.

A recently published report described the clinical experience in Japan.605 Notably, although neuromuscular block was not routinely monitored intraoperatively, the TOF ratio was determined after tracheal extubation. A total of 249 patients were studied in three separate groups. Patients with spontaneous recovery ( n = 23) were compared with patients receiving either neostigmine ( n = 109) or sugammadex 2.7 mg/kg ( n = 117) for antagonism of neuromuscular block. Although the sugammadex group had the lowest incidence of residual neuromuscular block, all three groups had an unexpectedly high incidence of residual neuromuscular block.605

A scholarly editorial written by Naguib and associates606 placed prime emphasis on the likely inadequate dose of sugammadex used for antagonism of rocuronium, in combination with the lack of qualitative or quantitative neuromuscular monitoring (neuromuscular block was managed using “clinical criteria”). Although proper monitoring is strongly advisable, an adequate dose of sugammadex is under current debate. And although the neuromuscular antagonist effect of neostigmine is not improved by increasing the dose above the recommended dose, there are

strong reasons to believe that sugammadex in doses larger than 2.7 mg/kg (the average dose used in the report)605 would have been more effective. Others argue that a dose of sugammadex larger than 2.0 mg/kg would not be necessary if proper monitoring were used. Of course, another possibility exists: a larger dose of sugammadex (if needed) based on quantitative neuromuscular monitoring would be ideal practice.

In conclusion, sugammadex provides a novel concept in reversal of neuromuscular block. Although the current price of sugammadex continues to be an important factor that may limit its use, many institutions currently use sugammadex for routine antagonism of neuromuscular block.
